CLR-0124 — Three Major New Proposals Added: Replication Matter, Megastone Attunement, Specimen System
Tag: ADD
Replication Affinity Matter Framework: 18 World Affinities proposed as genuine custom Replication (M.M.I.) matter types — Affinity becomes literal matter the world's material system understands, not just a stat. Replication's 6 remaining custom-matter slots deliberately left RESERVED (not filled) for non-Affinity phenomenon/state matter — candidates named but not locked: Corruption (Shadow research), Instability (Fractured research), Resonance, Memory, Life, Creation. Worked example: Shadow Fire research could resolve to a real Fire+Corruption matter signature instead of needing a whole separate invented currency
Blank Keystone → Megastone attunement: Mega Evolution's Keystone stops being crafted from a recipe and becomes something earned: attuning a Blank Keystone during a Pokémon's Full Resonance state permanently imprints that Pokémon's specific signature, becoming its Megastone. Reframes Mega Evolution as a relationship achievement. Deliberately left open: species gating, X/Y-variant determination, location/ritual requirements
Specimen system (Retirement Protocol expansion): Donated retired Pokémon become Cobblemon-to-Item Research Specimens, processed in a Replication Terminal for weighted-random research discoveries based on actual Matter Yield/Matter Profile (size/mass/condition/Nature/form/state all affect contribution, not a flat per-species value). Five-tier specimen taxonomy (Environmental/Biological/Remnant/Historical/Legacy) as distinct research categories, not a rarity ladder. Resolves the Nuzlocke-exclusivity gap: normal mode gets its own high-value specimen path via a reframed Ghost-mob addon (residual Resonance imprints of Pokémon that died outside the player's knowledge, rare Remnant Specimen drops) rather than either being locked out or soft-forced into Nuzlocke mechanics. Starting specimen proposed as a starter-choice-determined tutorial mechanism. Explicit scope guardrail preserved: generates information TOWARD resources, not resources directly — not a duplication backdoor
Reason: Wesley's extended design session, consolidated directly into the archive rather than staged in a separate handoff document, since this thread already is the Claude archive
Cross-references: → Part 6 (Replication Matter, Megastone); → Part 2 (Specimen system, Retirement Protocol)
Current Status: 🔵 Current proposals throughout — conceptually well-developed and consistent with already-locked systems, none mechanically implemented yet

What changed: Third verification pass on the Replication Affinity Matter proposal found two more genuine gaps: (1) the multi-matter-signature-per-item mechanic — a single item/specimen can carry multiple matter types with different weights simultaneously (e.g. 20 Affinity + 5 Living + 2 Resonance), which is what makes the two-axis Affinity+Phenomenon research grammar mechanically real rather than just naming; confirmed current on the actual target stack (KubeJS 2101.x, NeoForge 1.21.1). (2) Two companion mods for managing matter values at scale — Replication Extended Registry and Replicated Integration — added to M.M.I., both directly relevant to assigning the 18 Affinity matter values across many existing items without hand-editing Replication's own data
Reason: Wesley's continued verification against source material — fourth gap-finding pass across this same proposal thread
Cross-references: → Part 6; → M.M.I.; → CLR-0124 through CLR-0126 (prior integration passes on this proposal)
Current Status: 🔵 Current proposal, now fully captured across four verification passes
CLR-0128 — Four More Gaps Found Across This Session's ChatGPT Document Series
Tag: ADD
Replication-as-research-instrument philosophy (Part 6): The core reframe distinguishing Replication from a generic infinite-resource machine — it's a research instrument qualifying legitimate reproduction, not a duplicator. Includes the knowledge-progression ladder (UNKNOWN→IDENTIFIED→STUDIED→CHARACTERIZED→REPLICATION POSSIBLE→STABLE→REFINED, where the player's UNDERSTANDING levels up, not a machine tier), the Replication Fidelity concept (first successful replication isn't guaranteed equivalent to the original, giving a reason to keep researching post-unlock), and three explicit scope boundaries: Replication ≠ Synthesis, replication failures aren't automatically Shadow/Fractured, and Pokémon replication is a separate, much harder frontier than material replication
Extreme Synthesis anomalies (Part 2): Six candidate names for ultra-rare Synthesis outcomes beyond the normal taxonomy (Impossible Synthesis, Persistent Rejection, Recursive Synthesis, Multi-Synthesis, Domain Intrusion, Temporal Synthesis) — none locked, explicitly kept as discoveries rather than progression requirements. Flagged the terminology-collision risk against the event system's own 'Anomaly' (Living World Events), which needs its own resolution once one of these gets a final name
Standalone Ecological Resonance scope note (Authorities): Brief distinction worth preserving: 'Ecological Resonance' as a concept is broader than C.E.R. specifically — a possible future non-Pokémon derivative project, not an active one. Scope clarity only, no design content
Reason: Wesley's continued direct verification against the ChatGPT document series — fifth+ gap-finding pass this session, consistent with the established pattern of genuinely dense documents rewarding a second look
Cross-references: → Part 6; → Part 2; → Authorities Archive
Current Status: 🔵 Current proposals/candidates throughout, none locked

ATTUNEMENT COMPLETE.') as a tone reference point. Added to Part 6

Rather than a standing server setting (which sat awkwardly against D.O.P./no-power-creep), Boosters is reframed as C.E.R.'s dedicated system for TEMPORARY amplification — the short-lived counterpart to the project's permanent systems. Slots into the existing progression stack as the one missing piece: Affinity (long-term relationship) → Resonance (deeper interaction) → Resonance Aura (observable signature) → Type Gems/Liquid Affinity (crystallized/stored Affinity) → Resonance Hearts (permanent progression) → Fusion/Synthesis (permanent structural alteration) → Research (understanding/recording), with Boosters filling 'temporarily intensifying an existing phenomenon' specifically. Likely best gated to specific triggers (Restoration Festivals, Living World Events) rather than a raw timer, though that gating isn't locked yet

MODIFY | Something changed without being fully replaced

# SOURCE: ChatGPT_Reconciliation_Report.docx
---

C.E.R. — ChatGPT Archive Reconciliation
A dedicated comparison between this archive (the original, more current line) and ChatGPT's parallel archive track (Authorities/Change Log/Interaction Archives, Open Questions doc, Full Sweep Findings, Integrated Project Update, all uploaded Sept 2026). Nothing here is integrated yet — this is purely for review. Organized by significance: real conflicts first, then things ChatGPT's line predates/is behind on, then anything genuinely worth considering that isn't already covered.
1. Real Conflicts / Divergent Terminology (need a decision)
2. Where ChatGPT's Archive Is Behind (already resolved here, no action needed)
3. Genuinely New / Worth Considering (not yet in this archive)
4. Overall Verdict
ChatGPT's archive line is a legitimate, real parallel effort — but it started from this archive as its base and has since fallen behind rather than surpassed it in any area checked. Every substantive comparison this pass could find resolved in this archive's favor: either the same conclusion reached independently (a good confidence signal), or this archive already resolved something ChatGPT's line still lists as open. The Landmark Build Guides remain the one clear exception — genuine new value, already integrated.
Recommendation: no further reconciliation work is needed unless a future ChatGPT upload surfaces something new. This document can be treated as closed unless reopened by a new batch.
Item | This archive | ChatGPT's archive | Recommendation
Legendary discovery mechanism naming | Resonance Selection & Memory Phenomenon, triggered by an Echo Token | “Gumball Selector vs. Memory Echo” — different names for what appears to be the same underlying system, with an explicit note that the exact split between the two “counterpart systems” was never finalized on their side | RESOLVED (Wesley, Sept 2026): confirmed Gumball Selector/Memory Echo are the original, now-outdated terms; Resonance Selection/Memory Phenomenon are current. Locked into Part 6 as historical terminology record. This item is closed.
Resonance Machine naming | Resonance Processing / Resonance Machines is the core Poképower-driven system (Part 6) | ChatGPT's Change Log notes it used its own F.R.M. discretion to rename an unrelated ‘Gumball system’ secondary label AWAY from ‘Resonance Machine’ specifically to avoid colliding with this same term | No real conflict — both sides independently protected the same name for the same core system. Worth noting as a second confirmation, not integrating anything new.
Item | ChatGPT's status | This archive's actual status
World Affinity decay | Initially treated as potentially permanent/non-decaying in earlier material, later self-corrected in the Integrated Project Update | Resolved with far more nuance: two-phase model (CLR-0053, CLR-0055) plus the Sundering as the causal lore explanation (CLR-0054). ChatGPT's own later report actually caught up to this correctly — no conflict remains, just confirms this archive got there first and with more depth.
Ecological Apex Pokémon umbrella question | Still listed as requiring Alpha/Apex/Twisted/Shadow to ‘layer onto native Alphas’ without a resolved umbrella structure | Fully resolved (CLR-0045): Ecological Apex IS the umbrella, Titan Pokémon is a confirmed subfamily, Alpha is a fully separate phenotype axis. ChatGPT's line never caught up to this resolution.
Resonance Heart (material) vs. Resonance Hearts (progression) naming collision | Listed as an open, unresolved naming collision in the Open Questions doc | Already resolved on this side: material renamed to Resonance Core, then Resonance Nexus (Part 3/4). ChatGPT's Open Questions doc is working from a stale snapshot.
Scope/coverage | References only 10 Master Archive parts | This archive has 17 parts plus the Master Mod Index — substantially more developed, including the entire Drive restructure, Affinity Essence, Titan Pokémon, the Sundering, Enhanced Research Branches, and the full Content Consolidation Sweep, none of which appear anywhere in ChatGPT's line.
Item | Source | Assessment
Landmark Build Guides (World Tree, Observatory, waterfall valley, floating islands, town, ruins/bridges) | ChatGPT batch | Already integrated as Part 17 (CLR-0072) — confirmed genuinely new and non-duplicate before integrating. No further action needed.
Skill Tree specialization system detail (branch names/progression sketches) | ChatGPT's Full Sweep Findings + already-uploaded Part 11 | Cross-checked: this exact content is ALREADY in this archive's own Part 11, word-for-word similar structure. Likely both archives independently recovered the same source material rather than one copying the other. No new content to add.
Region-based danger tiers, Ditto Synthesis, Crafting Removal philosophy | ChatGPT's Full Sweep Findings | All three independently verified as already present in this archive (Parts 2 and 4), in equal or greater detail. No new content.

# SOURCE: Cobblemon_Deep_Dive.docx
---

Cobblemon — Full Feature Deep Dive
Compiled by Claude, September 2026, for C.E.R. review purposes. Sourced primarily from the official Cobblemon Wiki (wiki.cobblemon.com), cross-checked against CurseForge/Modrinth listings and community wikis. Covers Cobblemon core (mod version 1.8.0, “Make Your Move”, released Sept 6 2026) — not addons, which are separately tracked in the Master Mod Index. This is a standalone reference document, not part of the numbered Master Archive sequence.
Three parts: (I) every current feature the base mod offers, organized by system; (II) a version-history section covering major updates and why each change was made, in case anything superseded is worth reconsidering; (III) a rewards brainstorm — items, XP, food, cosmetics, Pokémon, and events that could work as rewards across C.E.R.'s various systems.
Part I — Current Full Feature Set
Organized by system. Where a feature ties to something already locked in C.E.R.'s own archive, that's noted — but this section is meant as a neutral inventory of what Cobblemon itself provides, not a design proposal.
1. Pokémon & Spawning
Over 340+ Pokémon across all generations implemented natively (Gen 1 complete at 148; later generations partial and still expanding every update — 1.8.0 alone added Gen 3–9 species and several regional forms).
Full IV/EV/Nature/Ability system, matching mainline mechanics exactly.
Showdown-accurate battle calculations — same damage formulas, type chart, and move data as the mainline games.
Biome-based wild spawning, with spawn tables configurable per biome/time/weather/moon phase.
Alpha Pokémon (added 1.8.0): bigger, stronger, more aggressive than normal Pokémon; always know a TM move; always lead herds; herds always contain at least one Alpha.
Intrinsic Pokémon size scaling (1.8.0): every individual Pokémon is randomly 5% smaller to 5% bigger than baseline, independent of any “Alpha” status; baby Pokémon additionally scale from 10% smaller at level 1 up to normal size by level 10.
Regional forms and Unique Forms (cosmetic pattern variants, e.g. Arbok's 14 pattern variants added in 1.8.0).
Shiny Pokémon with the standard rarity model, plus a separate “Gigantamax-style” and Mega-adjacent cosmetic variant space handled through addons rather than core (Mega Evolution itself is NOT in core Cobblemon — it's addon territory, e.g. Mega Showdown).
Herd spawning behavior — certain species spawn in groups with shared behavior.
Legendary/Mythical Pokémon with rare, gated spawn conditions (specific biome/time/weather requirements; some effectively unobtainable without addon-provided alternative encounter methods).
Fished-up Pokémon (via Poké Rod) and “surprise encounter” Pokémon (e.g. Gimmighoul) are now correctly marked “seen” in the Pokédex as of 1.8.0 — previously a bug.
2. Catching & Poké Balls
48 distinct Poké Ball types, each with a unique catch-rate modifier or special effect (e.g. Quick Ball: 5x catch rate on the first turn only).
Balls crafted from Apricorns (grown on Apricorn Trees) plus other Minecraft materials — ball crafting is integrated into vanilla-style crafting rather than a separate currency. Ball recipes are tag-based, deliberately making datapack/addon compatibility and replacement easier.
Ancient Poké Balls — a distinct sub-category with real physical differences, not just a reskin: Ancient Feather/Wing/Jet Balls fly farther when thrown, while Ancient Heavy/Leaden/Gigaton Balls are heavier and don't fly as far. A 1.8.0 fix corrected several of these not applying the correct capture multiplier.
Held-item visibility toggle for caught Pokémon, configurable via command argument as of 1.8.0.
3. Battling
Full turn-based battle system rendered directly in the Minecraft world — no separate battle screen/menu takeover; battles happen where you stand.
Wild battles (walking into aggro range or interacting), Player (PvP) battles, and Trainer battles (the latter requires an addon or datapack to actually place NPC trainers in the world — core Cobblemon provides the battle engine, not trainer placement).
Double, Triple, and Multi-battle formats — native since the 1.6.0 “Record Catch” update, not an addon feature. Battle spectating and explicit forfeit support also exist natively.
Free-movement-during-battle option (a Legends Arceus-style toggle): build, mine, or fight other mobs while a Pokémon battle is ongoing.
Move relearning and physically running from wild battles (also Legends Arceus-inspired).
Flat-level battling — set all participating Pokémon to level 50, 100, or 5 for the duration of a specific battle (added 1.6.0); flat-level battles specifically do not award normal experience/EVs during that mode.
Evolve-on-your-own-terms option — the player decides when a level-up evolution actually triggers rather than it being forced immediately.
NPC battle AI, usable by any addon that spawns trainer NPCs (core provides the AI, addons provide the NPCs) — as of 1.8.0 NPCs no longer switch Pokémon excessively often mid-battle (a fixed bug).
`moveset_builders`, `party_pools`, and `party_compositions` datapack folders (1.8.0) — lets addon/datapack authors define complex, weighted NPC party compositions rather than hand-writing every trainer's team.
4. Storage, PC & Party Management
Standard PC box system with multiple named/wallpapered boxes.
PC Wallpapers — cosmetic box backgrounds; 1.8.0 added alternate versions based on Cobblemon's original pre-release PC design.
Party overlay HUD showing live stat changes; as of 1.8.0 it also displays the actual amount of EXP gained in its animation.
Data Monitor block — can now (1.8.0) accept various “Disks” (TMs, Music Discs, Upgrade, Dubious Disc) to change its display, and can be used to evolve a compatible Pokémon into Porygon by inserting an Upgrade or Dubious Disc.
Pasture Block — lets Pokémon roam outside their ball on your land; performance was specifically improved in 1.8.0 for both singleplayer and servers, and the default wander radius was reduced to 32 blocks.
Trading, including Trade Evolutions (e.g. Link Cable-style trade-triggered evolutions) with proper validation (a 1.8.0 fix addressed improper trade validation); traded Pokémon now correctly retain their original friendship level when traded back to their original trainer.
5. Riding & Mounts
Native Pokémon riding, added in the 1.7.0 “Set Course” update — shift+right-click a rideable species and select the ride option. No saddle required.
Ten additional species made rideable specifically in 1.8.0: Crobat, Skarmory, Milotic, Archeops, Goodra (both standard and Hisuian form), Drampa, Duraludon, Archaludon, Pidgeot.
Multiple riding styles — land, liquid (submarine/surfacing), and air/hover categories, each with real physics differences, independently bug-fixed across versions (hover-mount collision/momentum issues and submarine surfacing animation issues were both specifically fixed in 1.8.0).
Each mount carries five real riding statistics — Speed, Acceleration, Skill, Jump, and Stamina — with different riding styles interpreting the same five stats differently depending on their movement type.
Riding distance is tracked cumulatively by category (land/air/liquid), feeding into statistics/advancement infrastructure.
Aprijuice (a Cobblemon-original consumable) can alter a specific mount's riding characteristics based on juice flavor and the Pokémon's own Nature.
Conditional ride seats (1.8.0) — seat availability can be gated by Molang conditions such as “is_alpha”, letting addon authors define ride behavior differently for Alpha-status mounts.
Configurable riding camera (“Disable Roll” option for motion sickness), saved per riding style; a 1.8.0 fix resolved the “Remember Riding Camera” config not being respected on dismount.
Hover-mount block-destruction penalty removed in 1.8.0, making them viable for digging.
6. Breeding — NOT natively in core
Important distinction: full breeding (egg groups, egg moves, IV inheritance, Nature inheritance, shiny-hunting via breeding) is NOT a base-Cobblemon feature — it's addon territory (Cobbreeding, Daycare+, and the shared Cobblemon Breeding API framework other breeding addons build on). Core Cobblemon does not include a Day Care mechanic on its own.
6a. Marks (cross-checked and expanded via a parallel research pass)
A full native provenance/identity system distinct from cosmetics or held items — mainline-game marks are represented, plus Cobblemon-original ones: fishing marks, personality marks, weather marks, and time-of-capture marks.
Partner Mark — added specifically in 1.7.2, tied to a relationship/bond context rather than a capture-method context.
Mini, Jumbo, and Alpha marks — all three added specifically in 1.8.0, alongside the Alpha Pokémon feature itself.
Marks can be toggled on/off for display in the summary screen.
Worth treating as a genuinely strong native reward category for C.E.R. specifically — a mark is identity/provenance, not power, which fits low-stakes reward design (Mental Economy Delegation) better than most other native reward types.
7. Fishing
Poké Rod — a modified fishing rod added in the 1.6.0 update; requires a Poké Rod smithing template plus a base fishing rod and a Poké Ball to craft. Different Poké Balls produce differently themed rods.
Pokémon and item fishing through the Poké Rod, including Lure Ball functionality (bonus catch rate for fish-caught Pokémon) and Repeat Ball functionality (bonus catch rate for already-registered Pokédex entries) when the corresponding ball type is used to build the rod.
Bait/Seasoning system (`Seasoning` items) affecting fishing outcomes; 1.8.0 changed the bait effects of several vanilla and Cobblemon berries/items and fixed odd stacking behavior with bait items.
A 1.8.0 fix closed an exploit where players could remove bait or drop a baited Poké Rod to avoid actually consuming the bait.
8. Cooking & Food
Campfire Pot — added in 1.7.0, crafted from Apricorns and placed on a campfire; used to cook Poké Cakes, Poké Snacks, regional specialties, and potions (less efficiently than a brewing stand), plus bait for fishing.
A large roster of Cobblemon-specific food items introduced alongside cooking (Ponigiri, Sinister Tea, and many others), each with real gameplay effects (healing, status curing, stat-boosting, friendship-affecting) rather than being purely cosmetic food.
Poké Snacks specifically can be used as bait to spawn/attract wild Pokémon, and certain berries used in a Poké Snack recipe can influence Hidden Ability outcomes on the resulting encounter — a real mechanical hook, not flavor text. Poké Cakes, by contrast, are decorative/consumable and do NOT spawn Pokémon.
Regional Foods specifically prioritize being EATEN by the player over being used to heal a Pokémon by default — shift-clicking overrides this to use them for healing instead (clarified/fixed in 1.8.0).
Pokémon have a fullness value; feeding increases it, and each food item contributes a different amount — this is the mechanical backbone behind food actually mattering rather than being purely cosmetic.
Extensive Farmer's Delight cross-compatibility (1.8.0): cutting-board interactions for Apricorn/Saccharine logs, Pep-Up Flowers, Medicinal Leeks, Tumblestone blocks, Type Gem blocks, and Hearty Grains; Farmer's Delight crops can be used in Cobblemon recipes (Open-Faced Sandwiches); Applin Apples work in both Cobblemon's and Farmer's Delight's apple recipes.
Create mod cross-compatibility for food/brewing production lines (Rage Candy Bar Mixing recipe, milling berries for dye, milling Big Root/Revival Herb into their powder forms, crushing ore/gem clusters, etc.) — extensive as of 1.8.0.
8a. AI, Ecology & Behavior (cross-checked and expanded)
Pokémon AI runs on Minecraft's Brain system — the same underlying framework vanilla villagers use — enabling genuinely complex behavior rather than simple state machines.
Pastured Pokémon can sleep, approach the player, and be instructed to attack hostile mobs specifically.
Certain species groups can intimidate specific vanilla mobs outright.
Some Pokémon hunt each other; some herd together; some owned Pokémon actively defend the player.
Combee specifically can gather nectar and deliver it to Saccharine leaves or beehives under documented conditions — a genuine small-scale ecological behavior loop already native to the mod.
Type/ability-specific environmental interactions exist: Lightning Rod, Motor Drive, and Volt Absorb all interact with lightning strikes per their normal game logic, and Ground-type Pokémon are flatly immune to lightning damage.
9. Fossils & Revival
Fossil Dig Sites — worldgen structures where fossils can be found; 1.8.0 improved several fossil structures' natural terrain-generation blending, and fixed a specific fossil-related structure-placement bug.
Resurrection Machine — revives fossils into their Pokémon; a 1.8.0 fix addressed a crash from adding enchanted items to it, and a separate fix addressed occasional crashes specifically on retrieving revived fossils.
10. Technical Machines (TMs) — new in 1.8.0
TM Machine — crafts TMs from elemental Type Gems; recipes are unlocked individually by actually having a Pokémon that already knows the move (a Discovery-Over-Prescription-flavored gate, notably).
`/givetm` command; config options for infinite-use TMs and instant-unlock of all TM recipes for servers that want a lighter-touch version.
A new “Move Dex” added to the Pokédex specifically to help players identify which Pokémon can learn a given move and vice versa.
Type Gem blocks, crystal clusters, and Deepslate Crystal Core blocks — all new worldgen/crafting infrastructure introduced specifically to support TM crafting.
New advancement triggers tied to TM learning: per-TM, all-TMs-learned, and a running count-based trigger, backed by a new persistent per-player stat.
11. Habitats — new in 1.8.0
Habitat Block — a spawner block intended for servers and adventure maps, letting map/server builders directly control what spawns in a defined area rather than relying purely on biome tables.
49 new habitat structures added alongside the Habitat Block feature — these generate rarer Pokémon than normal wild spawning, and some rotate which Pokémon can appear by day.
12. Structures & Exploration
Village Pokémon Centers — generate as part of vanilla village worldgen, giving villages a native healing/PC presence without needing an addon for basic Pokémon-center infrastructure.
Ruins — worldgen structures with loot; 1.8.0 added 10 new ruin variants, all of which (per a 1.8.0 change) now guarantee a Gimmighoul/Gilded Chest where they previously sometimes lacked one, and all gilded-chest loot tables now guarantee a TM not otherwise obtainable through any Pokémon's natural level-up moveset — each ruin has its own specific guaranteed TM.
Gimmighoul Towers — now generate at true ground level rather than partially sunk, as of 1.8.0.
Shipwreck Coves (a “Mini Dungeon” category) — including a new Magma Shipwreck Cove variant added in 1.8.0; coves now generate more frequently, have upgraded ominous-state enemy equipment (enchanted gear, automaton armor trim), and each cove type (Magma/Lush/Submerged) guarantees one of the three elemental Hyper Beam TMs in its main treasure chest.
Abandoned Fishing Boats — drop maps leading to Shipwreck Coves; as of 1.8.0 these maps intelligently skip already-explored chunks.
13. Cosmetics
A dedicated Cosmetic Item slot system for Pokémon, entirely separate from held items — lets a Pokémon wear a cosmetic without affecting its held-item slot (this is the same native system multiple addons researched this session, like Comfey Cosmetics and Pokopia, build on top of).
Numerous species-specific cosmetic unlocks tied to specific held items or interactions (e.g. Partner Caps, Dragonite's mailbag cosmetic — which as of 1.8.0 can also be triggered via a chest item in the cosmetic slot, not just the original trigger item).
Aspect/pattern-based cosmetic variation for some species (Arbok's 14 patterns, added 1.8.0, is the largest single example).
14. Commands & Technical/Addon-Facing Features
A full command suite (`/pc`, `/givetm`, `/giveallpokemon`, and many others) for server administration and testing.
Extensive Molang scripting support for addon/datapack authors — 1.8.0 alone added roughly a dozen new Molang functions (enchantment checks, starter-selection state queries, TM lock/unlock functions, Pokémon mark functions, loot-table spawning, and more).
A documented Addon Creation pathway (its own top-level category on the official wiki) — Cobblemon is explicitly designed to be extended by third-party addons rather than trying to be a closed, complete game on its own; this is the same extensibility this whole M.M.I. research effort has been drawing on all session.
Data Component support for species drops (e.g. custom_model_data tagging) and various other addon-integration hooks added incrementally across versions.
15. Localization & Accessibility
Actively maintained translations across at least ten languages (Japanese, German, Spanish, Ecuadorian Spanish, Portuguese, Brazilian Portuguese, Simplified/Traditional Chinese, Korean, Russian, per the 1.8.0 update alone).
A “Disable Roll” riding-camera accessibility option specifically called out for motion sickness.
Part II — Version History & Why Each Major Change Happened
Not exhaustive down to every bugfix (1.8.0's changelog alone runs to well over a hundred individual fixes) — this covers the major named updates and the headline features/design reasoning behind each, so that if C.E.R. ever wants to reconsider drawing on an older mechanic, there's a record of what it was and why it existed.
1.8.0 — “Make Your Move” (September 6, 2026)
Headline additions: Alpha Pokémon, intrinsic Pokémon sizing, TMs (crafted from Type Gems), and Habitats. See Part I above for full current-state detail on each — this entry focuses on the *why*.
Alpha Pokémon: introduced to give wild encounters more variance and a genuine “big, dangerous, worth noticing” encounter type without requiring a whole separate boss system — herds always led by one, always know a TM move, scale to outmatch the player's own party. Functionally, this retired the need for external Alpha-mod addons overnight, which is exactly why C.E.R.'s own archive already dropped its external Alpha dependency once this shipped.
TMs: explicitly gated behind actually having a Pokémon that knows the move already — a deliberate design choice keeping move-teaching tied to genuine discovery/collection rather than a flat currency purchase, philosophically adjacent to C.E.R.'s own Discovery Over Prescription principle even though Cobblemon itself doesn't use that language.
Habitats: added specifically to give map-makers and server admins fine-grained spawn control without needing a third-party spawn-control mod — directly relevant to any structure-based Pokémon placement C.E.R. might want (Resonance Sites, restoration-triggered spawn changes, etc.).
Pokémon sizing: a small-scale realism/variety addition — every individual Pokémon, not just Alphas, now has a little bit of visual individuality baked in by default.
1.7.0 — “Set Course!” (November 22, 2025)
Headline additions: Pokémon riding and cooking mechanics (Campfire Pot).
Riding: the single biggest traversal-relevant update in Cobblemon's history — explicitly framed by the dev team as “we are now a riding mod” in their own patch notes. This directly obsoleted the need for a dedicated third-party riding mod, which is exactly the reasoning already locked in C.E.R.'s own archive for retiring an external riding dependency once this version shipped.
Cooking: added specifically to give Apricorns (previously mostly just a ball-crafting resource) a second, ongoing use, and to give food a real gameplay role (healing, status effects, friendship) beyond vanilla Minecraft hunger.
Also included in this update: friendship-lowering mechanics for certain healing items were reintroduced after being previously removed — worth knowing about specifically because it's an example of Cobblemon's own team reversing an earlier removal after reconsidering it, the exact kind of pattern Wesley asked about for C.E.R.'s own history.
1.6.0 — “The Record Catch Update” (December 25, 2024)
Headline additions: Double/Triple/Multi-battles, fishing (Poké Rod), the modern Pokédex, and flat-level battling.
Double/Triple/Multi-battles: added to bring Cobblemon's battle system up to full mainline-game parity — previously Cobblemon only supported single 1v1 battles, which meant no addon could build double-battle content (like Battle Tower's Doubles mode, or Cobblemon Trainer Doubles) until this update existed as a foundation.
Fishing: added to give exploration/idle activity a Pokémon-specific angle rather than only vanilla Minecraft fishing, and to make Lure/Repeat Ball's flavor-text effects (bonus catch rate on fished/already-caught species) actually mechanically real rather than just lore.
Flat-level battling: added specifically to support competitive/tournament-style play (Battle Tower's Boss Mode and similar structured-challenge addons lean on this) without requiring every participant's Pokémon to actually be the same real level.
Earlier major versions (pre-1.6.0) — full history, cross-checked and expanded
A second, independent research pass filled this section in with real per-version detail rather than a general summary — worth having in full given how much of it maps to reusable ER precedent.
1.0.0 (Nov 2022) — initial public release. No further rationale stated for the baseline itself.
1.1.0 (Nov 2022) — new party GUI, sleeping/swimming AI, status indicators, HP labels, more shiny textures, advancements, improved large-entity pathing, friendship tuning. Stated reasons: improve usability, prevent pathing problems, rebalance friendship, improve compatibility/stability.
1.2.0 (Jan 2023) — new PC interface, a full battle-system rewrite, complete resource/datapack customization of models/textures/animations/spawning/presets. Stated reasons: faster battle loading, fewer bugs, shared-server-host compatibility, ~50MB mod-size reduction. This is the update that established data-driven customization as a first-class citizen — directly relevant precedent for building C.E.R. as a layer rather than a replacement.
1.3.0 (Mar 2023) — 101 new Pokémon, smoother Poké Balls, spawn caps, more held/evolution items, shoulder effects, shearing/milking. Stated reasons: spawn caps specifically added because unrestricted waiting caused excessive populations — a real precedent worth remembering for C.E.R.'s own population-density decisions.
1.4.0 (Oct 2023) — Pastures, trading, nicknames, mints, medicinal plants, berries/crops, a large Pokémon wave. Explicitly branded “Friends and Farms” — the update that broadened Cobblemon's living-world/farm identity, which is directly why Pastures read as compatible with C.E.R.'s restoration fantasy rather than a bolted-on system.
1.4.1 (Dec 2023) — battle spectating, Adorn/Farmer's Delight compatibility, Apricorn furniture/boats/signs, new held items, special Vivillon patterns, milking.
1.5.0 (May 2024) — Ruins/fossils, the Resurrection Machine, Ancient Poké Balls, Original Trainer data, Display Cases, Type Gems, Gilded Chests, Relic Coins, Hisui starters, forfeit support. Release theme was explicitly archaeology/revival — this is the single biggest source of native ER precedent in Cobblemon's whole history (restoration, archaeology, and physical preservation are all already native design patterns here, not something C.E.R. would be inventing from scratch).
1.6.0 (Dec 2024) — “The Record Catch Update”: the modern Pokédex, Poké Rod fishing, Lure/Repeat Ball fishing interactions, flat battles, Double/Triple/Multi-battles, new structures. See the full entry above.
1.6.1 (Jan 2025) — 21 more Pokémon, Galarica Nuts/Cuffs/Wreaths, Repurposed Structures compatibility, Chiseled Bookshelf support for the Pokédex, scan-range/hide-unimplemented config.
1.7.0 (Nov 2025) — “Set Course!”: riding, Campfire Pot cooking, Aprijuice, Poké Snacks, cosmetics, the Brain-based AI rewrite, herds, PC sorting/filtering/wallpapers, marks, hyper training, statistics. See the full entry above.
1.7.1 (Nov 2025) — new trades/recipes, riding FOV, configurable riding freelook — minor polish update.
1.7.2 (Jan 2026) — Saccharine furniture, the Partner Mark, decorative item blocks, riding stats, more tea/food effects, dispenser support.
1.7.3 (Jan 2026) — stability fixes, a server-join fix, a flying-stamina fix, a PC-take fix, custom-ball namespace texture support, localization work. Explicitly a minor/stability-focused release.
1.8.0 (Sep 2026) — “Make Your Move”: Alpha Pokémon, intrinsic sizing, TMs, Type Gem worldgen, the Move Dex, Habitat Block + 49 habitat structures, Porygon acquisition via Data Monitor, conditional ride seats. Current baseline.
A Removed/Reworked/Superseded concepts — worth knowing before reusing anything old
Cobblemon's own team has removed or substantially reworked several systems over its history. Worth a direct list, since a couple of these are genuinely relevant precedent for how C.E.R. should think about its own historical removals (see the RCT restoration this session for a parallel case).
Shoulder effects (built-in data) — removed from core species data in 1.4.0 specifically so they could be rebalanced; the underlying shoulder-mount EFFECT SYSTEM itself still exists and can be re-applied through datapacks. Not a full removal, a move from hard-coded to data-driven.
Shulker-shell Forretress — removed from core entirely in 1.7.0, explicitly “in favour of it being an addon” (confirmed directly against the official 1.7.0 changelog). A genuinely direct precedent: Cobblemon's own team decided unusual/niche forms belong in addon territory, not core — several fan datapacks (e.g. “W's Shulker Forretress”) now restore it exactly as an addon, which is the same shape as C.E.R.'s own addon-driven approach to unusual content.
Old battle implementation — reworked entirely in 1.2.0 for faster loading, fewer bugs, and shared-host compatibility. Nothing about the old internals is worth targeting; build against current APIs/events only.
Old spawn architecture — the spawner hierarchy and terminology were heavily refactored in 1.7.0. Any C.E.R. spawn-logic design should target the CURRENT spawning architecture, not older documentation/APIs.
Advancement set — 1.8.0 refactored advancements and removed several considered unnecessary; nearly all previously-earned advancements were reset for every player except the root “choose a starter” advancement. Don't assume old advancement IDs are stable across versions.
Base stats display location — used to live on the Summary screen, removed from there specifically because the Pokédex now provides them instead. A real example of consolidation for its own sake (one authoritative place for a piece of information) rather than the information being cut.
A pattern worth flagging directly
Cobblemon's own development history shows the exact same kind of reversal Wesley corrected in C.E.R.'s own archive this session (RCT being restored after being incorrectly marked dropped): the 1.7.0 changelog explicitly reintroduces friendship-lowering mechanics for several healing items that had been removed in an earlier version, described in their own patch notes as being ‘reintroduced.’ Removal isn't always final even in the source mod C.E.R. is built on — worth keeping in mind as a general precedent, not just a C.E.R.-specific lesson.
Part III — Rewards Brainstorm
Not scoped to any one C.E.R. system — a broad pool of reward *types* (not final decisions) spanning items, XP, food, cosmetics, Pokémon, and events, meant to be drawn from wherever a system needs a reward and doesn't have one yet (Contests, Rallies, Research milestones, Championship Protocol, Battle Tower floors, faction reputation, restoration milestones, etc.). Organized by category, each with a short note on what kind of moment it fits.
1. Items
TMs (especially ones NOT learnable naturally by any Pokémon's level-up moveset — Cobblemon's own ruin-loot design already does exactly this, a good precedent to borrow) — fits high-value, one-time rewards.
Evolution items/stones, including any C.E.R.-custom ones (Resonance-flavored evolution triggers) — fits Research/Discovery milestones.
Rare Poké Ball types, or C.E.R.'s own Resonance Ball tiers — fits Contest/Rally prizes, faction reputation tiers.
Ability Capsules/Patches — fits competitive-battle-adjacent rewards (Battle Tower floors, Gym victories).
Held items, including cosmetic-triggering ones (Partner Caps and similar) — fits collection/completion rewards.
Type Gems / Type Memories / Terra Shards / other C.E.R. material-family items — fits Research and Restoration-tied rewards specifically, since these already have locked verbs (Condense, Understand, Anchor) that give a reward real meaning rather than being generic currency.
Crafting materials unique to a location (per Cobblemon's own Loot Identity philosophy, already echoed in C.E.R.'s Loot Sweep rules) — fits exploration/discovery rewards specifically, not generic ones.
Apricorns (rare colors/varieties) — fits low-stakes, frequent rewards; fits the Mental Economy Delegation principle (small satisfying pickups, not everything needs to be huge).
Fossils, including guaranteed-trait Special Fossils (already in M.M.I.) — fits Research/Paleontology-flavored rewards.
Battle Points-style currency (Battle Tower's own model) for a dedicated shop — fits repeatable-challenge rewards specifically, distinct from one-time unlocks.
2. Experience & Progression
Direct Pokémon EXP/Exp. Candy — fits almost any reward context, the most universal option.
Player-side progression currency, if C.E.R. ends up with a Trainer Level or similar player-facing meter — fits milestone/achievement rewards distinct from Pokémon-specific EXP.
Research-point-style currency feeding directly into the Research system already locked in C.E.R.'s own archive — fits Observation/Discovery rewards specifically, keeping Research's own internal economy self-contained rather than mixing it with generic XP.
Friendship/Affection boosts — fits Pokémon-care-flavored rewards (Pet a poke, Cobblemon Buffs synergy already logged in M.M.I.).
Level-cap increases, if C.E.R.'s three-cap system (Part 2/4) ever wants an event-triggered exception rather than only its normal progression paths — fits major milestone rewards specifically.
3. Food & Consumables
Regional/rare foods, including ones with real gameplay effects (per Cobblemon's own cooking system) rather than purely cosmetic snacks — fits Contest/Rally/festival rewards.
Seasoning/bait items — fits fishing-specific reward contexts.
Sandwiches/dishes requiring rare ingredients — fits high-effort crafting-adjacent rewards.
Potions/status-cure items, including C.E.R.-custom Resonance-flavored brews if that space ever gets built out — fits utility rewards, less exciting but genuinely useful.
Poké Puffs/Poffins/Poké Cakes-equivalent items tied to Contest-style activities specifically.
4. Cosmetics & Display
PC Wallpapers, including C.E.R.-custom Affinity-themed ones — fits collection/completion rewards; low-stakes, purely expressive, fits Mental Economy Delegation directly.
Cosmetic Items (the native slot system, Part I above) — fits faction/regional/festival rewards, since many existing cosmetic addons already tie specific cosmetics to specific regional/thematic identities.
Ball Stickers/skins (already in M.M.I. via Cobblemon: Ball Stickers) — fits low-stakes collection rewards.
Trophies/statues for display (APS Trophies, Cobblemon Stone Statues — both already in M.M.I.) — fits Championship Protocol and major-achievement rewards specifically, tying back into the already-locked physical-display philosophy behind Occupied Pokéballs/Championship Hall.
Player cosmetics (capes, badges, trainer-card flair) if C.E.R. builds out player-facing cosmetic identity beyond Pokémon cosmetics — fits faction reputation and Path-completion rewards.
Custom paintings/decorative blocks (Cobblemon Paintings, already in M.M.I.) — fits settlement/restoration-completion rewards, since these read as environmental storytelling rather than a generic prize.
5. Pokémon
Guaranteed-encounter or guaranteed-capture rare/regional Pokémon — fits major milestone rewards; should stay rare per the already-locked principle that reward-based acquisition should feel like “I found something,” not “the only way to get this Pokémon.”
Guaranteed-trait Pokémon (shiny, high-IV, specific nature/ability) via Special Fossils-style mechanics — fits high-effort/high-investment reward contexts specifically.
Alpha-status Pokémon as a specific reward tier, distinct from a normal capture — fits Boss Mode/Battle Tower-adjacent and ecological-health-tied rewards, per the already-logged Alpha-as-ecological-evidence integration idea.
Eggs (if breeding gets built out) with pre-set favorable inheritance — fits Farmer/breeding-path-specific rewards.
Historically “found” Pokémon via Occupied Pokéballs-style loot (already locked, Part 2/10) — fits exploration/archaeological rewards specifically, distinct from a direct capture reward.
6. Events & Experiences
Living World Events (Migration, Resonance Bloom, Affinity Crisis, Restoration Festival — all already locked, Part 3) triggered as a reward rather than only occurring naturally — fits major community/faction-reputation milestones.
Temporary access to a normally-gated area/Domain — fits Recognition/Legendary-adjacent rewards specifically.
A one-time cosmetic world change (a specific NPC returning, a structure lighting up, a settlement visibly changing) — fits Restoration-tied rewards, and costs nothing mechanically while being the most thematically resonant option on this whole list given C.E.R.'s central “watch the world heal” fantasy.
A named/commemorated moment (a plaque, a record in the Field Journal, an entry in a settlement's history) — fits low-mechanical-cost, high-narrative-weight rewards; costs nothing to implement relative to its emotional payoff.
Rally/Contest hosting rights or similar social-standing rewards — fits faction reputation specifically, tying back to the already-locked Faction→creation-verb specialization idea.
A boss/Apex encounter becoming available — fits high-tier Research or Restoration-completion rewards specifically.
7. A frequency-tiered way to think about the whole list above
Cross-checked against a parallel, independently-researched reward taxonomy — worth adopting as an organizing layer over everything in sections 1–6 above, since it answers a question this list alone doesn't: not just WHAT to give, but HOW OFTEN.
Common: raw XP/Exp. Candy, basic held items, common crafting materials, regional/uncommon food.
Uncommon–rare: evolution items, marks and cosmetic aspects, specialized Poké Balls, Research/knowledge unlocks, faction reputation, storage/display unlocks.
Rare: TMs (especially ones not naturally learnable), specific/rare Pokémon, traversal upgrades, narrative/journal content.
Rare–legendary: guaranteed rare-species encounters, Legendary-adjacent content.
Major milestone only: world-state changes (a structure actually being restored, a habitat unlocking, a route opening) — this tier should almost never repeat, which is exactly what makes it feel earned.
8. Reward categories worth actively avoiding
A short list worth keeping in view specifically because C.E.R. has so many systems capable of generating rewards — easy to accidentally lean on the same weak patterns repeatedly across different systems without noticing.
Flat currency as the default reward for every single quest type — fine as ONE reward family, weak as the only one.
Permanent percentage stat boosts with no real tradeoff — creates invisible power creep.
Universal catch-rate increases that make specialized Poké Balls pointless — directly undermines the already-locked “no universally best ball” philosophy.
Random loot crates as the PRIMARY reward for major research or Legendary-adjacent milestones — fine as a minor/common-tier reward, wrong as the headline reward for something meant to feel earned.
Endless stacks of generic crafting materials standing in for actual story milestones.
Rewards that effectively force every player toward the same build/path.
Inventing a new meter/currency purely to have something to hand out — if a reward doesn't fit an existing system (Affinity, Research, Virtue, faction reputation, etc.), that's a signal to reconsider the reward, not to invent a tenth progression bar.
Given the Mental Economy Delegation principle already locked in Authorities Archive, it's probably worth keeping in mind that not every reward needs to be mechanically significant — some of the strongest options above (a wallpaper, a plaque, a returning NPC, a sticker) are valuable specifically because they're low-effort and purely expressive, which is exactly the kind of reward that keeps a long session feeling rewarding without every single unlock needing to move a progression needle.
Part IV — What Cobblemon Gives C.E.R. for Free vs. What Still Needs Custom Work
A direct gap analysis, cross-checked against a parallel research pass — genuinely the most actionable single piece of this whole document, since it turns the feature inventory above into a build plan rather than just a reference list.
The pattern worth internalizing from this table: Cobblemon is consistently strong on RAW MATERIAL (data structures, hooks, events, spawn control) and consistently silent on MEANING (what any of it should represent ecologically). That's exactly the shape of gap C.E.R.'s Restoration Engine already exists to fill — this table just makes concrete, system by system, where the dividing line actually sits.
C.E.R. Requirement | Native Cobblemon Support | Custom C.E.R. Layer Still Needed
18 World Affinities | Type system, Type Gems, spawning conditions, species data | Affinity values, progression, relationships, and world-state logic itself — Cobblemon has no concept of Affinity as C.E.R. defines it.
Ecological Balance | Herds, AI, habitats, spawn pools, food, pastures | The higher-level balance model actually linking populations to restoration state.
Biodiversity / Population Health | Species, spawn pools, habitats, herds | Population-health calculations and restoration feedback loops.
World Health | Structures, spawning, NPCs, crops, general world state | The global restoration state and its presentation layer.
Trainer Affinity | Friendship, marks, statistics, interactions | The player-specific affinity model itself.
Distorted/Twisted Pokémon | Forms/aspects/species-additions and spawn data as the technical hook | C.E.R.'s own anomaly logic and ecological-transformation rules.
Alpha Pokémon | Fully native as of 1.8.0 | None — use it directly rather than building a second Alpha system.
Fusion / Fractured / Shadow | Data-driven Pokémon aspects, species additions, and events provide technical hooks | The actual synthesis system, outcome rules, corruption logic, and UI — Cobblemon provides no synthesis architecture of its own.
Resonance Aura | Molang, particles, Pokémon/entity data, observation hooks | C.E.R.'s entire conceptual Aura/observation layer — native support is purely technical plumbing here.
Legendary Recognition | Legendary species exist natively; Domains must be custom | The eligibility/recognition weighting and narrative progression architecture.
Research | Pokédex, Move Dex, stats, scans, fossils, marks, commands/events | C.E.R.'s actual research questions, journal, and investigation-chain design.
Restoration events | Structures, NPCs, spawn rules, habitats, loot all exist to be changed | The Restoration Engine that actually changes those systems in response to player progress — nothing in Cobblemon does this on its own.

# SOURCE: Interaction_Archive.docx
---

Cobblemon: Ecological Resonance
Interaction Archive
Compiled by Claude, September 2026. Per the Authorities Archive, this archive covers what happened around C.E.R. — contextual and casual conversation, tangents that might later become relevant. Honest finding: this collaboration was overwhelmingly on-task. A dedicated search across all three transcripts for off-topic tangents, personal asides, and casual banter turned up very little that isn't either core design (already in the Master Archive) or process/governance (already in the Authorities Archive). This archive is short by evidence, not by effort — padding it out with restated design content would misrepresent what's actually here.
1. How the Collaboration Actually Read
The overwhelming majority of pt1–pt3 is direct, sustained game-design work — even the humor in the transcripts (the Shadow Rotom enthusiasm, the “PLUSHLOCKE” naming moment, various 😂 asides) happens inside design discussion rather than as separate off-topic conversation, and has been preserved in context within the relevant Master Archive sections rather than duplicated here. There is very little “chit-chat” to separate out from “work” in this project — the two were basically the same activity throughout.
2. Genuine Tangents & Process Discussion
On chronology and document order (pt3, project start)
Wesley raised a process question worth preserving in its own right: the four source documents were pasted in chronologically-correct order internally, but the documents/chats themselves may not be in true chronological order relative to each other, since there were no timestamps to go by. The resulting principle — reconstruct actual project chronology from evidence (references, terminology dependencies, explicit revision statements) rather than trusting document order or silently rewriting history to look clean — is the same principle captured as part of the Archive Preservation Principle in the Authorities Archive; this entry preserves the original conversational context that produced it.
A parked-and-resolved tangent
At one point Wesley flagged two items to “circle back to” mid-conversation: (1) keeping Legendary eligibility distinct from recognition so an appearance feels intentional, and (2) the Plushlocke/Gaucho plush challenges. Both were picked back up later in the same conversation and are now fully documented in the Master Archive (Part 2) — noted here only to preserve the fact that they were explicitly parked and returned to, which is a small but genuine data point about how this project handles its own backlog.
A stated future-implementation preference
Wesley indicated that once the project moves from design into actual implementation, he wants work announced and scoped one piece at a time — e.g. explicitly announcing when work starts on Affinity, when it's finished, and when the project moves to the next concept — rather than jumping between systems without a clear announced transition. This wasn't stated as a rule for the current archive-reconstruction work, but as a preference for the eventual implementation phase, so it's preserved here rather than in Authorities Archive's binding process rules.
The Recovered Drives story — where the project's real history became its own lore
Worth its own entry rather than being buried inside a system proposal, because Wesley specifically asked for it to be preserved somewhere findable: while confirming the 4 (later 5) canonical Resonance Drive names, Wesley recalled a 5th Drive — for power generation — that didn't appear anywhere in the recovered transcripts. Rather than assuming the memory was simply wrong, the working theory that emerged was the more likely one: the missing Drive was probably discussed in the part of the original conversation that was actually erased — the same real data-loss incident that is the entire reason this archive-reconstruction project exists (Part 10).
Wesley's own reaction, worth quoting directly rather than paraphrasing away its warmth: “omg your right, maybe we can introduce a 5th, 6th or even 7th drive under the 'the world remembers' tagline, like recovering lost info... See this is why its always great to have other heads working with you.” That became the Genesis Drives (Part 6) — the idea evolved further from its first “lost technology” framing into a cleaner ancestor/descendant relationship (Genesis Drives came first naturally, Resonance Drives were built afterward by studying them, echoing Mew's own role as the DNA-memory ancestor of all Pokémon, Part 2), but the origin story stayed exactly the same. The parallel isn't a forced metaphor bolted on afterward — the real event and the in-fiction mechanic share the exact same shape, which is rare enough in a project like this to be worth remembering as its own small story, independent of whatever the final Drive roster ends up being.
3. Personal Context
Wesley mentioned he hasn't personally used the Nuzlocke mod and has only played real Nuzlocke challenges on the official Pokémon games — offered as context while working through the Retirement Protocol design (Master Archive, Part 2), not as a broader biographical note.

# SOURCE: Master_Archive_Part1.docx
---

Cobblemon: Ecological Resonance
Master Archive — Part 1: Project Identity & Core Systems
Compiled by Claude, September 2026, under Free Reign Mode / TriSweep. Covers: project identity, the World Affinity system, the Personal (Trainer) Affinity system, Resonance, EV/IV training, and the custom-species/Fusion architecture. See the Authorities Archive for the status-tag legend and F.R.M./sweep rules governing this document. Later parts (Legendary Domains, breeding/life system, Pokémon variants, factions, mods) are in progress.
1. Project Identity
Name: Cobblemon: Ecological Resonance (C.E.R. / E.R.). Status: 🔒 Locked — chosen after extensive comparison against “Resonance of the Living World,” “The Living World,” “Reawakening,” and others. “Project Riispawn” is retained as an internal development codename, separate from the public title.
Core fantasy: the world isn't dead — it's damaged, dormant, and trying to heal. The player becomes an agent of ecological restoration; Pokémon, Resonance, exploration, research, and rebuilding all feed into one another rather than existing as separate feature trees.
The C.E.R./E.R. initialism doubles as a deliberate “Emergency Room” motif: the world is treated as a patient, restoration as treatment, Resonance as the diagnostic/connection mechanism, and regions can be formally classified by percentage (“Ecological Resonance: 37% — Status: Critical” → “100% — Status: Resonant”). Not meant to be advertised outright to players — an internal layer of terminology they can gradually notice.
Platform: Minecraft 1.21.1, NeoForge, Cobblemon, KubeJS-driven scripting layer, built as a distinct “Restoration Engine” layer on top of Cobblemon rather than trying to find one mod that does everything. Status: 🔒 Locked.
Core design principles: Creation Over Crafting (C.O.C.) and Discovery Over Prescription (D.O.P.) — see the Authorities Archive. An explicit anti-pay-to-win principle also applies to any monetization if the pack or a standalone spinoff is ever distributed.
2. World Affinity System
The world has 18 independent Affinity values, one per Pokémon type, each starting at 0. Affinity is a separate attribute from a Pokémon's normal Pokémon type — a Pikachu keeps its Electric type while separately carrying an Affinity (e.g. Storm) that interacts with Ecological Resonance systems, not the type chart.
0 doesn't mean a type is impossible to encounter — it means “the world has forgotten this type.” Rare remnants can still exist via special encounters, structures, and fossils even at 0.
World state by affinity value (0–999, with 1000 = fully Restored)
Status: 🟢 Strong reconstruction — this is the earliest full articulation of the system and stayed structurally consistent through later discussion, though exact numeric thresholds should be treated as tunable, not locked.
Wesley's explicit rationale for deferring exact totals (World and Personal Affinity alike): the total value has to be reachable through accumulation across many different fronts — world events, side activities, Affinity-based rewards, etc. — and individual reward sizes need to stay proportionate to that total (a total of 100 with 50-point rewards half-fills the bar in one task; a total of 3000 with .0001-point rewards makes every task feel pointless). Deciding the actual numbers before deciding how widely Affinity is dispersed and integrated across systems would mean picking numbers blind. Deliberately held open until that breadth is decided — not a gap, an intentional sequencing choice.
Ecological effect, not just a spawn switch
High affinity in one type actively reshapes what can live in the world via an Affinity Matrix of relationship weights (e.g. high Fire suppresses Grass/Bug/Ice spawn weight while barely touching Psychic/Normal/Fairy) — explicitly decoupled from Pokémon-type battle weaknesses, since ecological relationships and battle match-ups are different axes that happen to correlate sometimes, not always.
Final spawn weight is conceptually: Base Species Weight × Biome Weight × Type Affinity Modifier × Counter-Affinity Modifier × Restoration Modifier × Player/World Unlock Modifier. Suppressed types become ecologically unlikely, not forbidden.
How affinity is generated
Capturing matters far more than killing (encounter +0.1, defeat +0.5, catch +3, raise/evolve +5, restore habitat +50, as illustrative starting values) — the system is meant to reward care/restoration/preservation over farm-and-kill loops. Negative actions (extinguishing fires, killing Pokémon of a type, actively working against a habitat) apply small penalties, deliberately kept tiny so players can't accidentally wreck an ecosystem by routine play.
This decay applies to WORLD Affinity specifically — an ecosystem/regional value, not the player's own Trainer/Personal Affinity (see the Permanence rule below, which is the opposite: Personal Affinity never decays). World Affinity decays very slowly toward equilibrium (illustrative: ~1 point/Minecraft day) rather than requiring constant maintenance, and generation scales through three phases: Phase I — manual player interaction; Phase II — infrastructure (industrial furnace, aqueduct, irrigation network, power station generating affinity/day); Phase III — Stabilization, where a late-game Affinity Stabilizer locks an ecosystem's value so it stops decaying, i.e. permanently restored. Magic (e.g. Ars Nouveau rituals, Occultism spirit binding) was proposed as the stabilizing counterpart to Create/tech's generating role.
Resolves an interpretive disagreement worth recording: a follow-up ChatGPT pass read a later clarifying passage (“World Affinity should never require constant player maintenance”) as evidence the decay mechanic was never actually locked. Checked directly against that passage in full context: it's a refinement of HOW decay should feel (gradual, tied to sustained settlement/generation health, never an immediate punishing drop), not a reversal of decay existing at all — confirmed by an adjacent line describing a Fire Noble's defeat as making Fire “no longer require constant maintenance,” which only makes sense if maintenance was needed before that point. Wesley's direct call: keep the decay + Stabilizer mechanic as written above.
Two-phase decay model (Wesley, Sept 2026) — unifies World and Trainer Affinity under one logic
A refinement that resolves the whole decay question elegantly rather than picking one rule over the other: World Affinity follows two different phases depending on whether its region's Stabilizer has been activated. Phase 1 (pre-Stabilizer): the original ~1 point/Minecraft day decay toward equilibrium applies exactly as described above — nothing is anchoring the region's Affinity yet, so it naturally drifts. Phase 2 (post-Stabilizer): the moment a player activates that region's Affinity Stabilizer, the underlying Affinity value becomes locked/permanent — the same rule Trainer Affinity already has (see the Permanence rule below) — and a Resonance-equivalent layer for that region takes over as the thing that can still fluctuate with ongoing activity/neglect, rather than the core Affinity number itself.
This creates a clean, satisfying symmetry between the two systems instead of an arbitrary difference between them: both World and Trainer Affinity ultimately follow “permanent core value, fluctuating Resonance layer on top” — the only real difference is that the Trainer's permanence was true from the moment they earned it, while a region's permanence has to be actively built and earned through restoring and stabilizing it. Status: 🕒 Locked — this is now the governing model for World Affinity decay, superseding the flat “always decays until eternity” reading.
Lore payoff: the Sundering destroyed the Stabilizers (Wesley, Sept 2026)
A genuinely excellent connective piece, proposed directly in response to confirming the decay mechanic above: the reason the world is in such bad shape at the start of the game isn't simply “accumulated slow decay over unknown time” — it's that Affinity Stabilizers, which an ancient civilization had built and maintained to lock World Affinity in a restored state (Phase 2 of the two-phase model above), were destroyed during the Sundering (the working-name ancient war/catastrophe first proposed as an original C.E.R. backstory, inspired by real Pokémon canon patterns like the AZ/Ultimate Weapon story — not previously formally recorded in this archive until now). Every region the player finds has been forced back into Phase 1 as a direct result — not because it was never restored, but because it WAS restored once, and the thing keeping it that way was destroyed. That reframes the player's entire restoration arc: they aren't building something new, they're rebuilding something the world already had and lost.
This ties directly into Part 2's already-locked story payoff (the ancient civilization severing humanity's access to the Domains, sealing them off rather than destroying them) — the Sundering is the event that necessitated both: the civilization sealed the Domains AND lost the Stabilizers in the same catastrophe, which is why restoring the Stabilizer technology becomes a genuine late-game goal with real narrative weight (rebuilding lost civilization-level infrastructure, not just building new tech) rather than an arbitrary crafting unlock. Status: 🔵 Current proposal, genuinely strong — first formal record of the Sundering concept in this archive, directly confirmed and extended by Wesley.
Safeguards
Minimum ecosystem floors (e.g. a type cannot naturally fall below ~10% baseline) to prevent an irrecoverable death-spiral where a suppressed type's own suppression drives it further down.
Affinity refuges — protected locations (Ancient Grove, Frozen Sanctuary, Sacred Lake, Dragon Shrine, etc.) where a specific type's affinity has a raised floor, explaining why a suppressed type still survives somewhere and giving exploration a reason to exist.
A hidden World Balance value measuring how unevenly the 18 affinities are distributed — the true late-game objective isn't “restore all 18 to 100,” it's restore balance between them, tying into the implied mystery of why the ancient civilization removed Pokémon in the first place (possibly an attempt to control World Balance, not the Pokémon themselves). Status: 🟡 Candidate — evocative worldbuilding hook, not confirmed as locked story canon.
Visibility to the player
World Affinity is meant to be felt environmentally (embers appearing at 100 Fire, volcanic activity at 500, rare species at 900, etc.) before it's read numerically. An early central hub UI concept (“Affinity Observatory”, later folded into what the memory export calls the Restoration Observatory) shows all 18 as bar readouts; portable versions were proposed as a Resonance Compass (qualitative flavor text) upgrading to a Resonance Scanner (exact numbers) as a progression reward. Status: 🟡 Candidate/❓ needs verification against later Observatory naming — “Affinity Observatory” here vs. “Restoration Observatory” in later material appear to be the same concept under evolved naming. Buildable geometry (exact WorldEdit commands for the 18-pillar/pool ring): Part 17.
High-affinity encounters (“Nobles”)
At the originating point in the conversation, extremely high single-type affinity (750/900/1000) was proposed to produce rare species, then a special “Noble” encounter, framed as the spirit/guardian of a restored ecosystem rather than simply a stronger Pokémon; defeating/capturing/appeasing a Noble could stabilize that type's affinity outright. Status: ❓ Needs verification — later material (see Section 3 forward and the ChatGPT memory export) discusses Legendary/Mythical recognition and Domain Authority in terms that look like an evolution of this same idea; whether “Noble” survived as separate terminology or was fully absorbed into the Legendary Domain system isn't resolved in pt1–pt3 alone. Flagging for cross-reference in Part 2.
3. Personal (Trainer) Affinity System
Deliberately given a different job from World Affinity so both remain worth pursuing: World Affinity determines what the world can provide; Personal Affinity determines what the player can personally do with it. A player can be a Personal Fire specialist in a world that's still ecologically starved of Fire, or vice versa — neither meter can substitute for the other.
(World Affinity has its own separate milestone table — 10/25/50/75/90/95+/100 — governing ecosystem/species availability rather than player capability; see Section 2.)
Trainer Affinity progression philosophy (resolves an earlier naming ambiguity)
A follow-up deep sweep on Affinity Exposure recovered a third, genuinely distinct band-naming set, resolving what Part 10 had flagged as a possibly-duplicate of the World Affinity tier table above: this one is specifically Trainer/Personal Affinity's own qualitative progression, not a restatement of World Affinity's tiers (Section 2) or Restoration Stages (Part 12, the region/world-level Dormant→Recovering→Stable→Flourishing→Resonant scale). Conceptual bands: 0–20 Initial Awakening, 20–40 Recovery, 40–60 Stabilization, 60–80 Flourishing, 80–95 Deep Restoration, 95–100 Resonance Completion — explicitly conceptual framing rather than final UI labels.
Governing philosophy, worth preserving directly: Affinity should be paced by discovery and restoration depth, not resource accumulation — Early Affinity = participation, Late Affinity = understanding. The player shouldn't reach 100 in a given Affinity simply by repeating “catch 500 Fire Pokémon”; the final stretch should require discovering what that Affinity still lacks. Status: 🟢 Strong reconstruction — now confirmed as its own distinct concept across two independent sweep passes, not a naming collision with World Affinity's tiers or Restoration Stages.
Permanence rule, recovered: Affinity doesn't decay, Resonance does
A genuine correction recovered from the source material, previously missing from this archive despite being an explicit design decision: Trainer/Personal Affinity is PERMANENT once earned — “you earned it, you keep it,” full stop, no decay under any circumstance. This is deliberately distinct from World Affinity above, which DOES decay slowly toward equilibrium until a late-game Affinity Stabilizer locks it — the two systems behave differently on purpose, not inconsistently.
What actually responds to neglect is Active Resonance, not Affinity itself: Active Resonance can “cool down” if a Type goes unused for an extended period (e.g. spending weeks focused on Ice lets Fire Resonance drop even though Fire Affinity itself stays exactly where it was earned), and Dormant Affinity (an Affinity not currently active/selected) stays fully preserved but becomes less efficient until reawakened. The intended player feeling, stated directly in the source: not “oh no, I haven't farmed Fire today, I need to go grind,” but “I've been using Ice all week, so my Fire Resonance is getting cold — maybe I'll stop by the forge.” A gentle pull back toward variety, not a punishment for specializing. Status: 🕒 Locked — explicit design decision, recovered directly from source material rather than reconstructed.
Capacity, not a checklist
Players cannot maintain all 18 personal affinities at full strength — an Affinity Capacity limits how many can be Active at once (illustrative progression: 2 at the start → 3 early → 4 mid → 5 advanced → 6 endgame → 7–8 at mastery). Everything discovered beyond that capacity becomes Dormant — preserved, not lost, and drifting slowly toward a baseline unless stabilized. Changing the active loadout (“Resonate” to activate, “Release”/“Severance” to deactivate) happens at a Resonance Shrine / Attunement Chamber, with a resource/time/cooldown cost that eases as the player builds a proper Attunement Chamber later on.
Resonance Energy and type relationships
Active personal affinities feed a dedicated resource, Resonance Energy (distinct from vanilla XP), which governs max energy, regen rate, ability costs, and training bonuses. Type pairs fall into three relationship categories:
Harmonized (e.g. Water+Ice, Fire+Steel, Grass+Water, Psychic+Fairy, Ghost+Dark, Ground+Rock): active together, they add a synergy bonus (illustrative +15% Resonance Energy, +10% related ability efficiency) rather than just summing.
Neutral: no special interaction.
Conflicting (e.g. Electric+Ground): active together, they impose a penalty (illustrative -15% Energy regen, +10% ability cost) without being blocked outright — the player can still choose both, they just pay for it.
A late-game Affinity Imprinting mechanic lets a player preserve a small passive effect from a formerly-active affinity without it counting against their normal capacity, rewarding long-term investment without forcing every player into max-strength generalists.
Status: 🟢 Strong reconstruction for the overall shape (capacity, dormant/active, harmonized/conflicting, Resonance Energy). Exact numeric bonuses/penalties throughout this section are illustrative examples from the original brainstorm, not locked balance values.
4. Resonance (general concept)
Resonance is the central connective concept linking Pokémon, the world, types, the player, ecology, technology, resources, and restoration — explicitly not meant to function as merely another mana bar. It is the mechanism referenced throughout the Affinity systems above (Resonance Energy, Resonance Shrine, Resonance Compass/Scanner) as well as in Fusion (“Resonance Synthesis”, referenced in Section 5) and the eventual Resonance Hearts / Legendary recognition system covered in Part 2. Status: 🟢 Strong reconstruction (per the ChatGPT memory export, cross-checked against the transcripts); full mechanical definition (Resonance Signature, Resonance Aura, Resonance Synthesis specifics) is reserved for Part 2 given how deeply it's entangled with Fusion/Shadow/breeding, which hasn't been fully extracted yet.
5. Virtual EV/IV Training System
Rather than vanilla EV-grinding, the player uses a machine (working name: Battle Resonator, built with Create + magic) to run virtual training programs on a Pokémon.
EVs are deliberately easy: virtual, automatable, resettable with special items. IVs are deliberately hard: expensive, requiring rare materials and late-game magic/tech, possibly capped at certain stages, with a perfect IV treated as a genuine endgame accomplishment (e.g. a “Genetic Resonator” reforging one IV by +1 at high cost). Training facilities were proposed to interact with World/Personal Affinity — e.g. higher Fire affinity makes Fire-focused training cheaper — tying competitive Pokémon-building back into the restoration loop instead of decoupling it. Status: 🟢 Strong reconstruction; fully configurable numbers (exact EV gain, IV cost, training time, resource requirements) were explicitly left open for later tuning.
6. Custom Species / Fusion Technical Architecture
Confirmed technically feasible and mapped out in detail: Poke Fusion (the fusion mod) only needs to know a result's Cobblemon species ID — it does not need to understand or generate the model itself. The pipeline is: Poke Fusion recipe (two input speciesIds → one result speciesId) → Cobblemon looks up that species → the species' resolver points to its model/texture/poser/animations → Cobblemon renders it like any other Pokémon.
A custom Fakemon therefore needs: a species JSON (gameplay data — stats, types, abilities, catch rate, egg groups, etc.), a Blockbench model (.geo.json), texture(s) including a shiny variant, a resolver (the file that actually links species → model/texture/poser), a poser (idle/walk/battle/cry/faint animation bindings), and a lang file entry (name + Pokédex description). All of it should live under a dedicated namespace (e.g. ecoresonance:) rather than colliding with Cobblemon's own species IDs.
Proposed fusion categories to structure the eventual system: Genetic Fusion (two existing Pokémon), Affinity Fusion (elemental resonance-driven), Shadow Fusion, Restoration Fusion (ancient Pokémon restored via resonance), Fakemon Fusion, Triple Fusion, Corrupted Fusion (failed resonance — a dangerous result), and Perfect Resonance (extremely rare, legendary-tier). A single fused species can also carry its own aspects/visual variants (normal/shadow/stellar/perfected/unstable/awakened) via Cobblemon's existing resolver/aspect system, meaning the fusion result itself can have its own further progression rather than being a dead end. Status: 🕒 Locked for the technical pipeline (this is implementation-verified against actual Cobblemon/Poke Fusion documentation, not just a design idea); 🟡 Candidate for the specific fusion-category list, which reads as an illustrative menu rather than a finalized taxonomy.
Open threads for Part 2
Legendary Domains and how they relate to (or supersede) the early “Noble” concept above.
Resonance Hearts, Virtues, and the Legendary/Mythical recognition system (per the memory export, narrowed to ~8–10 Hearts corresponding to Virtues).
The life/retirement system (8 lives, retirement protocol, championship protocol).
Shadow forms, Eeveelutions, Fusion/Fracture failure states, and Corruption.
Breeding, materials (Type Gems/Plates/Drives, Terra Shards, Z-Crystals, Resonance Fossils).

100–249 | Stirring
250–499 | Returning
500–749 | Established
750–899 | Thriving
900–999 | Dominant
1000 | Restored
