# SOURCE: Master_Archive_Part14.docx
---

Cobblemon: Ecological Resonance
Master Archive — Part 14: Eeveelutional Resonance (Full System)
Compiled by Claude, September 2026, under Free Reign Mode. Eevee's role as the flagship demonstrator of Affinity Adaptation (Part 9, Part 13) turned out to be one of the single most-developed systems in the entire reconstruction — substantial enough that Wesley confirmed it deserves its own named research category, questline, and advancement set: Eeveelutional Resonance (locked, Part 13). This part is that system's full write-up, consolidating everything recovered about it. Three branches are locked as part of this system: Extra Eeveelutions (new Affinity expressions), Kazeran Eeveelutions (regional/environmental expressions), and Synthetic Eevee/Syn. Eevees (artificial adaptation).
1. Eevee's Core Role — Not the Protagonist, the Rosetta Stone
The central, load-bearing decision: Eevee is not the chosen Pokémon and should not become the mandatory starter, the mascot of every quest, or involved in every Legendary storyline. Instead: Eevee is the flagship example of what the Living World is capable of — “the Affinity Pokémon” or “Rosetta Stone” of the Affinity system, the first creature through which the player understands that environmental conditions and Affinity (and eventually Resonance) can alter Pokémon biology.
The single most important Eevee lore statement recovered, worth preserving verbatim: “Eevee does not uniquely possess the ability to adapt to Resonance. It merely demonstrates that ability more clearly than almost any other Pokémon.” Status: 🕒 Locked.
2. The Eevee Conundrum (named research mystery)
The research arc has an actual name: The Eevee Conundrum (preferred over “The Eevee Question” specifically because it should read as a scientific/ecological mystery, not a quest checklist). Progression of questions, deepening over the course of play:
Early: “Why does this Pokémon change differently depending on its surroundings?”
Middle: “Why are there so many different Affinity expressions?”
Later: “Is Eevee responding directly to Affinity, or is Eevee unusually capable of interpreting Resonance itself?” — deliberately left open as “The Eevee Conundrum” proper, preventing the simplistic lore of “Eevee evolves into a Fire Pokémon because Fire Affinity” in favor of “Affinity is the environmental signal; Resonance may be the underlying mechanism that lets Eevee interpret that signal.”
Eventually: “Can other Pokémon adapt in similar ways?” — answer: yes, but differently (leading into Section 3).
Final realization, the intended payoff of the whole arc: “Eevee isn't special because it was chosen. Eevee is special because it shows us what the Living World is capable of.”
A candidate answer to the Conundrum: Assimilation, not a secret stat
A proposed (not locked) resolution to what makes Eevee exceptional, tying directly into Part 9's Affinity Exposure/Assimilation material: rather than “Eevee has a hidden Assimilation stat,” the explanation could be that Eevee possesses an unusually receptive Resonance structure — most Pokémon merely Imprint an Affinity (Part 9: the mark, common), while Eevee assimilates it unusually readily (the incorporation, normally exceptional). That reframes the whole Conundrum as the player discovering a spectrum most Pokémon barely touch, with Eevee sitting unusually far along it, rather than Eevee having a unique gimmick nothing else shares. Status: 🟡 Candidate — a strong proposed answer, not confirmed as the final explanation.
Field Journal classification progression
Eevee's journal entry should NOT reveal “the Resonance Pokémon” early — that would spoil the mystery. Proposed gradual reveal: Observed Adaptability: Exceptional → Affinity Responsive → Affinity Adaptive → Resonance Receptive → Resonance-Compatible → [UNKNOWN], with the late realization being “Eevee wasn't changing because the player told it to. Eevee was listening to the world.” Status: 🟢 Strong reconstruction — the whole Conundrum arc, structure, and payoff line are well-developed; the exact quest-by-quest content is not yet designed.
3. The Adaptation Taxonomy & the Six Teachers
A proposed Field Journal research classification for how ANY Pokémon responds to Affinity/Resonance, turning Pokémon themselves into the textbook the player learns the system from:
Six named flagship “teachers,” each demonstrating a different adaptation archetype (this table already existed partially in Part 13; consolidated and confirmed here):
Intended learning progression across the whole taxonomy: Adaptation → Environmental Resonance → Interface → Deliberate Attunement → Corruption (Shadow). Status: 🕒 Locked for the taxonomy and all six archetypes.
4. Extra Eeveelutions (Branch 1 of 3 — New Affinity Expressions)
The 11 Extra Eeveelutions [Cobblemon] addon is the primary Fakemon candidate set, chosen because the official 8 Eeveelutions already cover Water/Fire/Electric/Psychic/Dark/Grass/Ice/Fairy, and these 11 cover the remainder — together spanning all 18 Affinities plus a culmination form.
Governing acquisition philosophy, explicit and important: don't homogenize the Eeveelutions into 18 identical “world affinity 100 + friendship” recipes. A 10-category acquisition toolbox was proposed (not mandatory categories, a design toolbox): Environment-dependent, Affinity-dependent, Research-dependent, Difficult-to-discover, Extremely rare, Material-dependent, Resonance-dependent, Event-dependent, Breeding-dependent, Hybrid/multi-condition — with hybrid conditions treated as the NORMAL case, not the exception. Every Eeveelution should have a reason to exist AND a reason to be obtained the specific way it is. Status: 🕒 Locked for the full roster, roles, and acquisition philosophy (this branch, as a whole, per Part 13); 🟡 Candidate for the exact numeric requirements per Eeveelution, which remain integration designs, not finalized mechanics.
5. The Standard Eight Eeveelutions
Notably less individually developed than the Extra Eeveelutions — Vaporeon (Water), Jolteon (Electric), Flareon (Fire), Espeon (Psychic), Umbreon (Dark), Leafeon (Grass), Glaceon (Ice), Sylveon (Fairy). Explicit direction: don't redesign their identities wholesale — preserve their existing/canonical evolution methods and integrate E.R. mechanics where logically useful, treating them as the baseline cases against which the Extra Eeveelutions read as increasingly unusual expressions of the same underlying principle. Status: 🟠 Acknowledged gap — system-level reasoning is locked (they ARE part of the 18-Affinity spectrum), individual per-Eeveelution E.R. integration is not yet developed to the same depth as the Extra roster.
6. Kazeran Eeveelutions (Branch 2 of 3 — Regional/Environmental Expressions) — LOCKED
Confirmed and locked as part of Eeveelutional Resonance (Wesley, Sept 2026) — previously sitting at “investigate.” Kazeran Eeveelutions are regional/adapted forms of the existing 8, not new species the way the Extra Eeveelutions are — explicitly distinguished as “new Affinity expressions” (Extra) vs. “regional/environmental expressions” (Kazeran), not redundant with each other. Example dual-type reinterpretations from the addon: Vaporeon Water/Fairy, Flareon Fire/Dragon, Jolteon Electric/Ground, Espeon Psychic/Steel, Umbreon Dark/Flying, Leafeon Grass/Ghost, Glaceon Ice/Fire, Sylveon Fairy/Poison. Status: 🕒 Locked as an included branch; 🟡 Candidate for exactly how its regional forms integrate with the Standard Eight's own (thin) E.R. mechanics.
7. Synthetic Eevee / Syn. Eevees (Branch 3 of 3 — Artificial Adaptation) — LOCKED
Confirmed and locked as part of Eeveelutional Resonance (Wesley, Sept 2026) — previously a candidate/investigate-only integration proposal. Cobblemon - Syn. Eevees adds Synthetic Eevee plus eight Synthetic Eeveelutions (Syn. Vaporeon, Syn. Jolteon, Syn. Flareon, Syn. Espeon, Syn. Umbreon, Syn. Leafeon, Syn. Glaceon, Syn. Sylveon). The addon's own lore: synthetic/robotic Eevee bodies originating from research into Pokémon behavior outside human interaction, later repurposed to give Eevees synthetic bodies after their original lives were cut short — unusually appropriate to E.R.'s themes without any rewriting needed.
This completes a three-way philosophical structure across all three branches, worth preserving in exactly this form: Natural Eevee — “My environment changed me” (Affinity Adaptation). Synthetic Eevee — “Humankind changed me” (artificial intervention). Resonance-driven Eevee — “Resonance changed me” (a proposed third state, not yet mechanically distinct from the other two, but philosophically anticipated). Explicitly NOT “robot Eevee for fun” — positioned as a genuine research question: can humans reproduce what the Living World does naturally? Connecting to Research, C.O.C., Resonance Processing, genetics, and Resonance Synthesis. Licensing note: CC-BY-NC-SA-4.0 — must be respected if assets are ever modified/redistributed. Status: 🕒 Locked as an included branch and the three-way philosophical frame; 🟡 Candidate for exact mechanics.
8. Eevee's Connections to Other Locked Systems
Eevee + Resonance Fusion (proposal, not locked)
A further possible role: choosing Eevee as the Anchor Pokémon (Part 13) in a Resonance Fusion attempt, with the proposal that Eevee might prove unusually receptive to accepting foreign Resonance signatures — a further piece of evidence for the Eevee Conundrum, suggesting Eevee's exceptionalism extends beyond its own evolution into general Resonance receptivity. Status: 🟡 Candidate — explicitly still a proposal, not a locked mechanic.
Eevee + Affinity Relationships
Hiveon (Bug+Grass) and Automeon (Steel+Electric) are the clearest existing demonstrations that some Eeveelutions represent a relationship BETWEEN Affinities rather than a single Affinity in isolation — making Eevee a way to teach Affinity Relationships (Part 9) through a Pokémon rather than only through an abstract UI concept.
Mutation framework (historical, distinguishes New Evolution vs. Variant Evolution)
An earlier, broader concept proposed merging Eeveelutions into a two-branch Mutation framework: New Evolution (“an evolutionary path nature never recorded” — e.g. a Rock/Dragon/Ghost Eeveelution, mapping to the Extra Eeveelutions) vs. Variant Evolution (“an existing evolutionary path behaving differently” — e.g. Water/Fairy Vaporeon, mapping to Kazeran). Status: 🟠 Historical/superseded as its own framework — the distinction itself survives, but is now expressed as the Extra-vs-Kazeran branch split (Sections 4 and 6) rather than a separate Mutation system.
9. Status Summary
Per the source material's own self-assessment, still broadly accurate: core role, Affinity Adaptation concept, the Eevee Conundrum, the research arc outline, the Extra Eeveelution lineup and individual roles, the acquisition philosophy, the 18-Affinity integration concept, Eeveeon, and Evereon are all strongly developed. Now additionally locked as full branches: Kazeran Eeveelutions and Synthetic Eevee. Still genuinely unfinished: individual canonical (Standard Eight) E.R. roles, exact technical evolution implementation, and exact visual/model design for the Fakemon forms — real implementation work, not conceptual gaps.
Adaptability class | Meaning
Fixed | Little environmental adaptation
Responsive | Behavior changes
Form-responsive | Physical form changes
Affinity-adaptive | Type/expression changes
Resonance-receptive | Can deliberately interact with Resonance
Exceptional | Unusual/unknown adaptation
Pokémon | Archetype | Question it teaches
Eevee | Exceptional/Adaptive Evolution | “Affinity changes what I can become” — one species, many expressions
Castform | Environmental Adaptation | “My environment changes how I manifest”
Silvally | Artificial/Attuned Adaptation | “Affinity can be deliberately mediated” (Type Memories, Part 3)
Rotom | Resonant Interface | “Resonance can connect biology to technology” (Create, Drives, Shadow Rotom, Part 2)
Burmy/Wormadam | Environmental Form | “My surroundings become part of my physical expression” — deliberately the simplest example
Mimikyu | Convergent Adaptation | “Different organisms can arrive at similar solutions” — Eevee's thematic opposite (one→many vs. many→one)
Eeveelution | Type | Proposed E.R. role
Spectreon | Ghost | Emotional/spiritual adaptation — Ghost Affinity + high friendship + haunted environment. Teaches that adaptation isn't purely physical; Resonance can respond to emotional bonds and death-associated places.
Acideon | Poison | Environmental toxicity adaptation — Poison Affinity + contaminated/swamp ecosystem + Poison Pokémon relationship. Teaches that a damaged ecosystem produces different adaptations than a healthy one.
Champeon | Fighting | Training/mastery adaptation — Fighting Affinity + demonstrated combat/training achievement + catalyst. Teaches that adaptation can be behavioral/earned, not just passive.
Aereon | Flying | Atmospheric adaptation — Flying Affinity + high altitude/wind exposure + flight catalyst. A clean physical-environment demonstration.
Minereon | Rock | Geological/resource adaptation — Rock Affinity + underground/mining environment + ore interaction. A textbook C.O.C./D.O.P. example: the player discovers Eevee responding to geology rather than crafting a Rock Stone.
Hiveon | Bug | Ecological symbiosis/pollinator adaptation — Bug Affinity + healthy flowering habitat + Bee/Beedrill interaction, possibly requiring Grass Affinity too. Demonstrates Affinity Relationships directly (Bug+Grass reinforcing each other).
Automeon | Steel | Technological/artificial adaptation — Steel+Electric Affinity + Create machinery/redstone + attuned material + research. The bridge between living adaptation and artificial Affinity Attunement; teaches that the same Affinity affects living and non-living systems differently.
Wyveon | Dragon | Rare/exceptional adaptation — Dragon Affinity + very high friendship + extreme environment + ancient Dragon research + a specific Resonance phenomenon. Deliberately one of the hardest — a discovery, not a farm.
Dungeon | Ground | Extreme-environment/survival adaptation — Ground Affinity + deep underground + prolonged darkness + lantern/light interaction. Teaches that adaptation can mean surviving an extreme environment, not just thriving in a pleasant one.
Eeveeon | Normal | Natural maturation — exceptional friendship + remaining unevolved for a long period + Last Resort + accumulated life experience. The philosophical counterpoint: “what happens when Eevee doesn't adapt into something else” — it adapts by becoming more completely itself.
Evereon | Special/culmination | Complete Adaptation — NOT simply “defeat all 18 Eeveelutions” (the addon's native requirement, explicitly reconsidered). Proposed E.R. requirements instead: knowledge of all 18 Affinities, experience with major Eeveelution pathways, substantial Personal/Trainer Affinity development, discovery of Affinity Relationships, witnessing/recording adaptations, extremely high Eevee development. The philosophical endpoint of the whole research arc, not just ‘the 19th type.’

# SOURCE: Master_Archive_Part15.docx
---

Cobblemon: Ecological Resonance
Master Archive — Part 15: Starting World & Starter System
Compiled by Claude, September 2026, under Free Reign Mode. A dedicated sweep for starting-world and starter material turned up a substantially richer system than previously captured anywhere in this archive. One critical honesty note carried through this entire part: the ORIGINAL six starting-world names/details were never recovered with confidence, and this part does not fabricate them. What follows is split clearly between recovered concept, a later six-Journey proposal, and a separate five-world historical proposal — none of which should be quietly treated as the lost original.
1. The Fundamental Two-Axis Idea
Starting choice was conceived as two separate axes: Starting World/Journey (where and under what circumstances you begin) and Starter Pokémon (what personally resonates with you). World + Starter + Player Actions = the actual personalized playthrough. This split exists specifically so the starter never becomes a permanent Pokémon-type class — a Torchic player can still end up heavily Ghost/Fairy oriented later. Status: 🕒 Locked.
2. The Six Starting Worlds — What's Actually Recovered
Strongly established: a six-starting-world concept existed. Explicitly NOT recovered with confidence: the original six names/details. This archive will not fill that hole by treating either proposal below as the original six — that would misrepresent a real gap as settled history. The worlds were meant to differ in starting environmental condition, initial Affinity biases, available Pokémon, starter possibilities, early resources, early restoration opportunities, NPC/community circumstances, and early gameplay emphasis — while never functioning as separate games. Status: 🔴 Major gap — explicitly acknowledged, not filled.
3. The Six-Journey Proposal (later reconstruction, not proven original)
Six was deliberately chosen to cover the pack's major pillars (Nature, Industry, Water/Infrastructure, Magic/Resonance, Mystery/Occult, Exploration/Frontier) without turning the opening into “pick one of 18 Affinity bars.” These do not replace the 18-type system — they're the player's introduction to it. Status: 🟡 Candidate — a strong later proposal, explicitly not proven to be the original six.
4. The Five-World Historical Proposal (a separate, earlier draft)
A concrete five-world list existed at one point: Verdant Frontier, Iron Frontier, Resonant Vale, Forgotten Marches, Frontier. This is its own historical artifact — not necessarily the same thing as the original lost six, and not the same thing as the six-Journey proposal above. The design exploration moved from five toward six specifically because six better covered the major pillars. Preserved per the Archive Preservation Principle as its own step in the lineage: 5-world proposal → evaluated → expanded to 6 Journeys. Status: ⚪ Historical.
5. The Starter Roster (eight starters, well-developed)
Three Gen 3 starters were deliberately included (Treecko/Torchic/Mudkip) — not because Gen 3 was superior, but because they formed a coherent foundation while the other five created genuinely different identities.
Individual reasoning
Treecko — preferred over Bulbasaur specifically for its wild-restoration feel, agility, and an evolution line (Treecko→Grovyle→Sceptile) read as small beginning→growing capability→mastery. Bulbasaur: considered, not selected.
Torchic — Fire deliberately reinterpreted as rebuilding (warmth/cooking/forging/industry), not destruction. No specific rejected alternative recovered.
Mudkip — Water/Ground bridges Nature↔Infrastructure directly; opening idea: damaged aqueduct as the first restoration lead.
Ralts — bridges Pokémon+emotion+Resonance+magic; proposed idea that the Observatory reacts differently to a Ralts because it senses the phenomenon differently, not because it's stronger.
Magnemite — a deliberately non-traditional starter tied to Create/railway/power. Proposed opening: a dead railway station, Magnemite approaches the old electrical system, a single click brings a light back on — the player's first genuine restoration moment.
Riolu — represents Fighting/courage/discipline/factions without primarily elemental-ecology or magic identity.
Eevee — tied directly to the pack's evolutionary mystery (Part 14's Eevee Conundrum) rather than popularity; “What can this Pokémon become?” mirrors “What can this world become?” A proposed (not locked) special mechanic: Eevee's Personal Affinity development could respond more strongly to player experience than other starters.
Litwick — explicitly called “the risky one.” Deliberately not “Litwick is evil” — framed as “a little light that remained after everyone else left.”
Status: 🟢 Strong reconstruction for the full roster and reasoning; 🟡 Candidate for exact mechanics.
6. Starter Determines Initial Generation Access (not permanently)
The starter sets which Pokémon generation is initially unlocked — confirmed examples: Treecko/Ralts→Gen 3, Riolu→Gen 4, Litwick→Gen 5, plus earlier examples Charmander→Gen 1, Rowlet→Gen 7, Froakie→Gen 6. Two players entering the identical world can encounter completely different Pokémon because their personal generation eligibility differs.
Critically, this is a starting point, not a permanent class: “This is where my journey begins,” not “I am the Gen 3 player forever.” Generation unlocking is meant to be part of the restoration story rather than a catch-count gate — proposed (not finalized) examples: Gen 2 via restoring an ancient research station, Gen 3 via a major exploration expedition, Gen 4 via a mysterious shrine, Gen 5 via faction standing, Gen 6 via a magical research milestone, Gen 7 via an ancient island/biome, Gen 8 via an industrial network, Gen 9 via a major world event. Status: 🕒 Locked for the mechanism; 🟡 Candidate for the specific unlock conditions.
7. Starter Also Sets Initial Personal Affinity Bias
Not a permanent class, not a large bonus — just the beginning of a Resonance relationship. Illustrative (not final) example values: Treecko (Grass 25, slight Nature resonance), Torchic (Fire 25), Mudkip (Water 20, Ground 10), Ralts (Psychic 15, Fairy 15). Status: 🟡 Candidate for numbers, 🕒 Locked for the principle.
8. Starter Changes the Opening Story (a different door, same world)
None of these permanently lock the player out of the others — they're opening perspectives, not separate campaigns.
9. World + Starter = Replayability (the actual point of the whole system)
Explicitly desired: same world + different starter = different experience, AND same starter + different world = different experience. Worked examples given directly: Iron Frontier + Magnemite → railway → power station → Create → industrial faction → electrical anomaly → Observatory. Iron Frontier + Ralts → Resonance → Ralts reacts → magical disturbance beneath the machinery → ancient Resonance conduit → Observatory. Forgotten Marches + Magnemite → dead machinery → Magnemite detects dormant electricity → ancient facility → corrupted machinery → Observatory. Same Magnemite, different world, different story.
Explicitly rejected: five/six completely different campaigns (“five modpacks in one”). The resulting architecture: Universal Story × Starting World Module × Starter Module × Player Behavior = Personalized Campaign. Summarized directly: “World determines circumstances. Starter determines perspective.” Status: 🕒 Locked as a design principle.
10. New Game+ / Resonant Beginnings (starter unlocks, not power unlocks)
Completing the pack unlocks new starting POSSIBILITIES, not a stronger start. Early proposal table (illustrative): Restore the World → Mew starter; Master Industry → Rotom; Master Magic → Special Ralts; Discover the Unknown → Special Eevee; Defeat the Great Anomaly → ???; Complete Everything → Unique Fakemon.
This later expanded into Resonant Beginnings: complete world → new starter; restore all 18 Affinities → new starting condition; restore all Hearts → advanced opening; Legendary recognition → Legendary-related possibility; resolve factions → faction-independent start; discover Fakemon → Discovery starter; resolve a Shadow crisis → Shadow-related alternate start; full completion → secret starting scenario — culminating in The Reawakening / Resonant Origin, where a fully-completed player chooses World + Starter + Opening Philosophy freely. Status: 🟡 Candidate throughout — real proposals, not locked mechanics.
10a. Reference Precedents — How Other Packs Solve This (research, not canon)
Live research (Sept 2026) into existing modpacks specifically for two unsolved questions this Part leaves open: how a pre-world-selection hub can work mechanically, and how a large branching questline gets structured. None of this is C.E.R. design — it's precedent to draw from, kept clearly separate from anything locked above.
StoneBlock 4 — the closest real precedent to the Six Worlds concept
Has almost exactly the shape Part 15 leaves unsolved: a pre-selection hub (“Garden of Stone,” underground, amethyst-lit) exists before the player commits to anything, from which they choose 1 of 5 themed starting locations genuinely differing in resources, lighting, and difficulty (a “classic stone box” up through a deliberately harder “Dark and Mysterious Cave” with minimal light). An NPC guide (Echo of Guidance) walks the player through their first tasks and hands over the quest book after one intro quest. Everything then feeds into one central structure — the World Engine, gradually restored across 15+ multiblock upgrades, tied to 6 Foundation Chapters with branching questlines (full completion isn't required to advance, just to get the best rewards/lore). A secondary loot layer (Vaults — handcrafted dungeons, each testing a different skill: logic, combat, endurance) runs alongside the main quest rather than gating it.
The structural mapping to C.E.R. is direct: Garden of Stone → the pre-Journey moment; the 5 themed bases → the Six Worlds/Journeys (Part 15, Sections 2–4); Echo of Guidance → a Resonance-flavored equivalent (a faded custodian, or the player's first Resonance Signature encounter); the World Engine → already filled by the Observatory (Part 12).
FTB Quests — the underlying technical architecture
The actual quest engine behind StoneBlock 4, FTB Evolution, and most Cobblemon questpacks. Real hierarchy: Chapter Groups → Chapters → Quests → Tasks/Rewards. Real dependency system: a quest can require one specific prior quest, or use a “minimum required dependencies” setting creating an OR condition (complete 2 of these 4 to unlock the next). Per-chapter Linear vs. Flexible progression-mode toggle — Linear locks task order strictly, Flexible lets the player work unlocked tasks in any order.
FTB-Cobblemon-Quests — a real, open-source Cobblemon-specific precedent
A real, MIT-licensed FTB Quests questpack built specifically for Cobblemon (162+ quests, designed for short/mid/long play sessions). Its progression shape: basic biomes → rare biomes → master all biomes → dimension exploration, alongside a full “catch one of each of the 18 types” branch — a near-structural-twin to C.E.R.'s own 18-Affinity system, worth studying directly for how it paces 18 parallel catch-goals without the whole thing feeling like a checklist.
FTB Evolution — checked, weaker precedent
A large kitchen-sink tech/magic pack (Mekanism, Just Dire Things, an FTB Pyramid endgame). No pre-selection hub or themed-starting-location mechanic found — confirms StoneBlock 4's hub system is a StoneBlock-specific innovation, not a standard FTB pattern.
ATM10 / AllTheMons — checked, not the relevant precedent for this specific question
ATM10's “Spawn Hub” turned out to be a specific community server's own hosting feature (warps, information boards), not a base-modpack mechanic — ATM10 itself is a standard single-overworld start with progressive dimension unlocks (Deep Dark → Mining Dimension → further dimensions via specific unlock items). AllTheMons (ATMons, the real ATM-team pack combining ATM10 with Cobblemon) follows the same pattern: “start with only a tree,” no themed-hub selection. Useful negative result — confirms StoneBlock 4, not the Cobblemon-crossover pack, is the actual precedent worth studying for this specific mechanic. Also incidentally confirmed Legendary Monuments (Part 4's evaluation) is genuinely used by the AllTheMons team for their own legendary content, independent confirmation the mod is real and actively relevant in this exact space.
Status for this entire section: 🟢 Strong, current, verified research — none of it is C.E.R. canon, all of it is available to draw from whenever the Six Worlds mechanics (still 🟡 Candidate, Section 3 above) get their real design pass.
11. Status Summary
🕒 Locked / 🟢 Strong: the two-axis World/Starter split; starter affects initial Personal Affinity and Generation access without permanently locking either; World+Starter should create genuinely different openings; the same starter should feel different in different worlds and vice versa; the shared core objective; starter selection should feel like an in-world Resonance event; the full 8-starter roster with documented reasoning.
🟡 Candidate: the six Journey identities and their exact Affinity distributions; exact starting structures/regional layouts/quest modules; exact numerical starter bonuses; exact generation-unlock quests; exact New Game+/Resonant Beginnings unlocks.
🔴 Missing: the actual original six starting-world names/details. This gap is preserved honestly rather than papered over.
Journey | Primary/Secondary/Possible Third | Identity
The Wilds | Grass / Water / Bug-Flying-Fairy | Nature winning — rapid ecological recovery, Ranger-oriented opening
The Hearth | Fire / Steel / Electric | Rebuilding civilization — partially functioning settlement, railway, forge, Industry faction
The Waters | Water / Ice / Ground | Waterways, infrastructure, agriculture, exploration
The Resonance | Psychic / Fairy / Ghost | Magic, mystery, ancient knowledge — supernatural phenomena earlier than other starts
The Forgotten | Ghost / Dark / Poison | Ruins, death, ancient history, corruption — Occultism-oriented, “Why did this world become empty?”
The Frontier | Electric / Steel / Fighting-Rock-Ground | Establishing (not restoring) civilization — exploration, survival, possible Paradox/Ultra Beast phenomena
Starter | Type | Gen | Role
Treecko | Grass | 3 | The Restorer — wild ecology
Torchic | Fire | 3 | The Builder — industry/warmth/rebuilding
Mudkip | Water/Ground | 3 | The Infrastructure Starter — waterways
Ralts | Psychic/Fairy | 3 | The Resonance Starter — magic/empathy
Magnemite | Electric/Steel | 1 | The Engineer — technology
Riolu | Fighting | 4 | The Challenger — training/factions/courage
Eevee | Normal | 1 | The Unknown — discovery/adaptation
Litwick | Ghost/Fire | 5 | The Forgotten — mystery/death
Starter | Opening perspective
Treecko | Recovering forest / ecological lead
Torchic | Forge / rebuilding
Mudkip | Aqueduct / water infrastructure
Ralts | First strange Resonance phenomenon
Magnemite | Dead railway / electrical system
Riolu | Old Battle Hall / faction training grounds
Eevee | Evidence that evolution itself has changed
Litwick | An abandoned shrine that shouldn't still be burning

# SOURCE: Master_Archive_Part16.docx
---

Cobblemon: Ecological Resonance
Master Archive — Part 16: Cobblemon & Pixelmon Feature Audit (Claude's Independent Pass)
Compiled by Claude, September 2026, via live web research (not from training data alone, given how fast both mods move). Produced independently, before reviewing Wesley's parallel ChatGPT audit, per his explicit instruction — comparison/integration with that document happens in a follow-up pass.
1. Cobblemon Version History (1.0.0 → 1.8.0)
This progression is unusually favorable for C.E.R.: 1.4 (Apricorns/farming), 1.5 (fossils/archaeology), 1.6 (research-adjacent Pokédex/fishing), 1.7 (Marks, living AI behavior), and 1.8 (Alphas, Habitats, Type Gems) each land on ground this archive had already been independently designing custom systems for.
2. The Single Biggest Finding: Habitat Blocks (1.8.0)
Cobblemon 1.8 added a Habitat Block — a spawner-style block controlling what spawns in an area, paired with 49 habitat structures (34 placeable via /locate, 15 as pure biome features), with some habitats varying which Pokémon appear by in-game day. This is close enough to what Part 12's Restoration Engine was already being designed to need that it changes the recommended implementation path: rather than building ecological spawning entirely from scratch, World Affinity → Ecological State → Restoration Stage could directly drive which native Habitat definitions are active in a region, letting Cobblemon's own spawning engine do the heavy lifting underneath C.E.R.'s ecological logic. Status: 🟢 Strong integration candidate — real, current, confirmed native to 1.8; exact data-driven flexibility for dynamic (not static) habitat swapping still needs hands-on testing.
3. Type Gems Are Now a Native Growing Resource
1.8 added naturally-generating Type Gem crystal formations — growing in stages from a Deepslate Crystal Core, farmable, occurring for all 18 types, used in TM crafting, droppable from Alphas, and found in ruin loot. This doesn't conflict with Part 3's Affinity Gem material family; it gives it a literal in-world growth mechanic to build on top of rather than needing a fully custom generation system. Proposed D.O.P. angle: “why does this crystal respond to this Pokémon?” → Research → Affinity → Resonance → refine into Affinity Gems for Resonance machinery. Status: 🟢 Strong integration.
4. TMs Are Already Close to Discovery Over Prescription
1.8's TM system uses Type Gems for crafting and unlocks recipes based on which Pokémon in the player's possession already know the relevant move — functionally: observe a move in use → the game recognizes you understand it → you can now produce it. That's remarkably close to D.O.P.'s existing philosophy without needing new infrastructure. Status: 🟢 Strong integration candidate.
5. Native Alpha Pokémon Retires an Old Dependency
Confirms and closes CLR-0002 (Change Log Archive): Alphas are fully native to 1.8 — larger, stronger, more aggressive, always know a TM move, lead herds, have unique cries/visual effects. The external Alpha mod dependency remains correctly retired. The Ecological Apex Pokémon concept (Part 7's open question) should layer onto native Alphas rather than reimplementing exceptional-individual mechanics from zero — Alpha + Apex + Twisted/Shadow as stackable traits (already the leaning answer in Part 7) fits native Alphas cleanly as one layer among several, not a replacement for the others.
6. Marks Are Now a Real Technical Target
Cobblemon's native Mark system (introduced 1.7, expanded with Mini/Jumbo/Alpha Marks in 1.8, Partner Mark in 1.7.2) makes Part 13's Resonance Marks concept a genuine technical integration target rather than a purely conceptual one — the underlying data structure to attach marks to a Pokémon already exists natively; C.E.R. would be adding new mark definitions with narrative weight (First Resonance, Echo-Touched, World's Memory) rather than building the whole system from scratch.
7. Riding, Cooking, and Living AI Behavior (1.7)
1.7's Brain-based Pokémon AI (herding, social behavior, sleep, defensive behavior) is arguably more important to the Living World premise than any single item system — it's the difference between static spawn tables and Pokémon that behave like they belong to an ecosystem. Cooking (Campfire Pot, Poké Snacks) and Riding both give C.E.R. native systems to build Affinity-flavored variants on top of rather than adding entirely parallel food/traversal systems. Status: 🟢 Strong integration — especially the AI behavior layer, which underlies the entire “World Is Alive” pillar.
8. Pixelmon Comparison — Confirmed and New Findings
Pixelmon's actual Shrine mechanic, verified directly: three shrine types (Frozen/Articuno, Static/Zapdos, Fiery/Moltres), each requiring a fully-charged corresponding orb (charged by defeating 375 Pokémon of any type) to summon its Legendary. This exactly matches what this archive already has on record — confirms Part 13's Resonance Sites lineage note is accurate, not misremembered.
New finding, not previously in this archive: Pixelmon has a full 23-quest onboarding research chain (Your First Catch through Champion's Path) covering catching, storage, berries, Apricorns, breeding, evolution, held items, fossils, shiny hunting, boss hunting, raid dens, and legendary tracking — essentially a built-in tutorial-as-research-tree. Worth comparing directly against Part 3's Research/Field Journal design, since Pixelmon has apparently already solved “how do you teach a huge system through discoverable quests” at a scale C.E.R. hasn't yet detailed.
New finding: Pixelmon's endgame unlock for the Tera Orb offers three alternate research paths the player can choose between (a Psychic/Fairy photography path, a boss-defeat path, and an Ancient-City-discovery path), with only one completable per player. This is a genuinely good structural precedent for C.E.R.'s own “multiple paths to the same Affinity awakening” principle (Part 12) — Pixelmon already ships a working example of exactly that design pattern.
9. Comparison with Wesley's Parallel ChatGPT Audit
Reviewed after completing the independent pass above, per Wesley's instruction. The two audits converge strongly on nearly every major point — both independently identified Habitat Blocks, Type Gem growth, TM/D.O.P. alignment, native Alpha, and Marks as the top integration priorities, and both reached the same core recommendation: Cobblemon should be the ecological/biological substrate, with C.E.R. supplying the Affinity/Resonance/Research/Restoration layers on top. That level of independent agreement is a strong confidence signal for all of it.
What the ChatGPT audit found that this pass didn't
1.2.0 “Customization” (Jan 2023) as its own milestone — major battle-system rewrite plus full datapack customization of models/textures/spawning, which this pass's version table skipped over.
Move Dex (1.8) as a distinct feature from the Pokédex — shows learnable-move relationships, directly useful for D.O.P.
Specific historical removal precedents worth preserving as design lessons: Forretress's Shulker-shell form was removed from core in 1.7 in favor of an addon (precedent: not every special form needs to live in core); the 1.8 advancement refactor reset most progress except the starter root (caution: don't build critical C.E.R. progression on fragile native advancement IDs without a migration plan); 1.6 removed interchangeable first-stage Hisuian starter evolution results for being too ambiguous (design lesson directly relevant to Part 14/15's starter and Eeveelution branching: if the player can't understand the consequence of a choice, make it an explicit discovery/branch instead).
Pixelmon's precise current version: 9.4.0, released Aug 17, 2026, on NeoForge 21.1.248 — more precise than this pass's general confirmation.
The Resurrection Machine's exact required formation (Data Monitor + Fossil Analyzer + Restoration Tank) — useful as prototype architecture for Resonance machinery.
A genuine priority matrix (High/Medium-High/Medium) across implementation candidates, more actionable than this pass's prose-only recommendations.
New proposals worth banking (explicitly candidates, not canon)
The ChatGPT audit's “NEW PROPOSALS” section is strong and non-redundant with anything already locked — worth adding to this archive as candidates rather than losing them in a second document:
Ecological Census — tracks observed species/population/herd/habitat/migration data as a research layer, explicitly not another mandatory numerical bar.
Habitat Restoration Score — an internal regional score gating which native Habitats can appear, visible to the player only through environmental evidence rather than a UI number.
Resonance Site Classes — Shrine/Altar, Ruin, Natural Phenomenon, Laboratory, Fossil/Temporal Site, Living Habitat, rather than forcing every Legendary into one identical structure type. Directly resolves one of this archive's own standing cautions (Part 9's audit: “not every Legendary gets a shrine”) with an actual taxonomy instead of just a rule against uniformity.
Field Observation Streaks — repeated non-destructive observation of a species/habitat builds research confidence without requiring capture.
Alpha Ecology — defeating/removing a population-leading Alpha could have real downstream ecological consequences in the custom layer, without touching native Alpha mechanics.
Affinity-affected Type Gem growth — local Affinity could influence a Type Gem's growth speed/yield/quality, making geology physically responsive to World Affinity rather than just thematically associated with it.
Research specimen provenance — fossils, marks, caught-ball history, origin location, and Resonance events all contributing to one Pokémon's research history, rather than being separate unconnected data points.
Restoration-visible spawning — restoration should gradually shift habitat composition, density, herd frequency, and rare-species eligibility, rather than simply toggling species IDs on/off.
Status for all of the above: 🟡 Candidate — explicitly proposals from the audit process, not decided design.
10. Recommended Next Steps
Compare this pass against Wesley's parallel ChatGPT audit (already uploaded) — reconcile, cross-reference, and integrate findings from both, same process as prior parallel full-sweeps.
Hands-on test Habitat Block data flexibility — can it be driven dynamically by a KubeJS/datapack hook reading World Affinity state, or is it closer to a static per-structure definition?
Investigate Pixelmon's 23-quest onboarding chain in more detail as a structural model for Part 3's Research/Field Journal progression.
Decide whether Cobblemon's TM/Type-Gem system should be layered under Affinity Gems directly, or kept as a parallel vanilla-adjacent system Affinity Gems merely interact with.
Version | Date | Name | Major additions

1.1.0 | Nov 27, 2022 | The Swim and Sleep Update | Party GUI, sleeping/swimming AI, status indicators, shiny textures
1.2.0 | Jan 1, 2023 | Customization | Major battle-system rewrite; full datapack/resource-pack customization of models/textures/animations/spawning — the technical foundation that makes a custom C.E.R. layer plausible without replacing Cobblemon (added per comparison with Wesley's parallel audit, see Section 9)
1.3.0 | Mar 24, 2023 | The First Steps | Expanded Pokédex, first PC system, complex animations
1.4.0 | Sep 30, 2023 | The Friends and Farms Update | Friendship mechanic, Apricorn farming/colors for Poké Ball crafting
1.5.0 | Feb 2024 | The Ruins and Revival Update | Fossil revitalization, ancient-themed Pokémon, archaeology foundation
1.6.0 | Dec 25, 2024 | The Record Catch Update | Pokédex item, Poké Rods/fishing, Lure/Repeat Ball functionality, Double/Triple/Multi-battles, flat-level battling

# SOURCE: Master_Archive_Part17.docx
---

Cobblemon: Ecological Resonance
Master Archive — Part 17: Landmark Build Guide (Six-Zone Scene)
Recovered from a parallel ChatGPT conversation, Sept 2026, and integrated after verifying it's genuinely new content (Full Sweep confirmed nothing else in that batch was). Practical build guidance for realizing the pack's signature scene in an actual Minecraft world: the World Tree, the Restoration Observatory (ritual plaza), the waterfall/river valley, floating islands, the restored town, and connective ruins/bridges. Not game-mechanical content — this is asset/build documentation, kept in the Master Archive because it directly operationalizes locked lore (the Observatory, Part 1) into buildable geometry.
1. Zone Breakdown
The World Tree — the central landmark; ancient, glowing, magical
The Restoration Observatory (Ritual Plaza) — the circular ruin with 18 pillars and pools, already locked in Part 12 as the Observatory; this guide gives it literal buildable geometry
The Waterfall/River Valley — the water system connecting everything, flowing from the tree's roots through the scene
The Floating Islands — scattered elevated landmasses framing the sky
The Town — the restored settlement, warm and lived-in
The Ruins & Bridges — connective tissue: crumbling walls, overgrown fragments, bridges tying the other zones together
2. Scale & Proportions
Forced perspective note: true epic scale isn't required if the camera sits close. Build everything ~30–40% smaller than the table above and shoot from nearer the subject — a screenshot won't reveal the difference, and it saves substantial build time.
3. Material Palettes by Zone
Keeping the Observatory and the ruins/bridges on the SAME stone-brick crumbling palette is what visually ties them together as one lost civilization rather than reading as unrelated structures — worth being consistent about that mix ratio across both.
4. Suggested Build Order
Phase 1 — Foundation & terrain: carve floating-island undersides and rough masses; terrace the river valley (bed, banks, waterfall drops); flatten the Observatory's and town's building pads.
Phase 2 — Major structures: World Tree trunk and root buttresses; Observatory rings, pillars, and pools (exact commands, Section 6); bridges connecting town/plaza/valley; town building shells (unpainted/undetailed).
Phase 3 — Detail pass (the highest-leverage phase for the final look — don't rush it): World Tree canopy, glow veins, root archway detail; waterfalls, pool vegetation, mist; crumbling detail on pillars/ruins; town interior lighting, window glow, gardens, paths; floating island surface planting and root/vine anchors.
Building in this order avoids being blocked waiting on an unfinished zone, and puts the detail pass — what actually sells the final screenshot — last, once every zone already exists to react to.
5. Tool Guidance
WorldEdit, biggest wins: large terrain shaping via `//brush sphere` in overlapping strokes (far more organic than manual placement); the Observatory's rings via hollow `//cyl` commands (Section 6); copy/paste symmetry — build ONE pillar+pool, bridge segment, or building, then `//copy`/`//rotate`/`//paste` repeatedly rather than hand-building repeats.
Structure blocks: save a finished template (pillar, house, bridge segment) as a reusable structure so later variations start from a known-good base.
Where an existing community tutorial beats written instructions: the World Tree and floating islands (both extremely well-covered topics on Minecraft build YouTube — real-time brush technique teaches faster than prose); stone arch bridges (arch-curve math is fiddly in text, trivial to copy from video); fantasy village/town builds (timber-frame and thatched-roof techniques are much easier to see than read).
Where there's no template to fall back on: the Observatory's 18-Affinity-colored pool ring is specific enough to this project that no tutorial will match it — exact commands are given below specifically because this is the one genuinely original piece of geometry here.
6. Restoration Observatory — Exact WorldEdit Commands
Three concentric rings: outer ring (radius ~24–26) with 18 pillars and pools, middle paved ring (radius ~14–16), inner raised dais (radius ~6–8).
Stand at the intended center point:
//cyl mossy_stone_bricks 26 1 — outer ring floor edge
//cyl stone_bricks 24 1 — paved floor, repeat with decreasing radius to fill toward center
//cyl smooth_stone_slab 8 1 — inner dais surface
For the 18 evenly-spaced pillars (WorldEdit has no native radial-array command): build one pillar + pool at due north, 24 blocks out from center; //pos1 / //pos2 around just that piece, //copy; //rotate 20, //paste — repeat 17 more times (18 total placements, 20° apart), always rotating around the same center point.
Pillar design: 7–9 blocks tall, 2×2 or 3×3 cross-section. Stone bricks with ~30% cracked stone bricks, ~15% mossy stone bricks, chiseled stone bricks as decorative bands every 3rd row. Remove the top 2–3 rows unevenly for a crumbled look rather than a flat cap. Vines on the shaded face, scattered rubble at the base.
The 18 pools: one per pillar, 2×2 or 3×3, one block deep. Rim each pool in the concrete/terracotta color matching its Affinity (orange for Fire, blue for Water, tan for Ground, etc.) — ties the Observatory directly to the 18-badge arc in the project's visual identity and gives real thematic weight beyond decorative ruins.
Vanilla-only alternative (no WorldEdit): use a free online Minecraft circle-block-chart for the three ring radii, and place the 18 pillars manually using (radius × cos(20° × n), radius × sin(20° × n)) for pillar n's offset from center.
7. The World Tree
The hardest element — organic, huge, needs to read as ancient/magical rather than “big brown blob.” Trunk: 60–90 blocks before canopy, tapering from 12–16 block diameter at the base to 6–8 at the top — don't build a perfect cylinder. `//brush sphere` (radius 6–10) dragged upward in overlapping strokes gives a far more organic trunk than `//cyl`; follow with a smaller brush for irregular bark gouges. Material: dark oak log base, ~20% spruce log/stripped dark oak mixed in for bark variation, moss climbing the shaded side. Root buttresses: 4–6 thick ridges extending outward/downward into the ground, tapering and partially submerging — this sells “ancient” more than anything else; some should arch up into walkable archways.
The glowing energy veins (the signature magical detail): carve thin 1-block channels into the bark following the grain, filled with light blue stained glass, sea lantern, or (best) amethyst blocks/bud clusters — amethyst gives a genuine soft purple-blue glow and reads as “crystal veins in bark.” If Ars Nouveau or Create end up in the confirmed mod list, both have glowing decorative blocks that fit thematically even better. Run veins from the roots into the lower canopy for continuity — the tree's light should read as coming from inside it.
Canopy: build at 3–4 height tiers rather than one dome, matching a layered/asymmetric silhouette. Mix: dark oak leaves (60% base), flowering azalea leaves (20%, color variety), moss carpet on top-facing surfaces, glow lichen or light blue glass panes tucked between clusters for light-filtering. Use `//brush sphere leaves` in 8–12 overlapping clusters at varying radii (4–10 blocks) rather than one giant sphere — a single sphere reads as a lollipop. Leave visible gaps for light rays and depth; canopies are never solid.
8. The Waterfall Oasis
Pool: ~50–70 block diameter, irregular natural shoreline (never a perfect circle), 4–8 blocks deep toward center. Add lily pads, kelp/seagrass, and prismarine/mossy outcrops breaking the surface.
Waterfalls: source from root buttresses and elevated terrain rim — 3–5 separate falls reads better than one giant one. Place a horizontal row of 4–8 source blocks for a wide fall rather than one single block. Break long drops into 2–3 tiered ledges (a “staircase” waterfall) for a misty, layered look and natural spots for secondary pools.
Mist/atmosphere: vanilla has no real fog blocks. Two honest options — a shader pack (Complementary or BSL, free) is the single highest-impact thing for the whole scene, not just the oasis (volumetric fog, god rays, water reflections); or vanilla-legal fake mist via barely-submerged soul campfires near the base of falls, giving a persistent rising-smoke effect.
9. The Floating Islands
Shaping: `//brush sphere` (radius 8–14) for the main mass, then a second smaller/randomized pass carving the underside into an irregular, jagged, “torn from the earth” silhouette — the underside should look broken, not sculpted. Keep tops relatively flat/rolling for soil and small trees.
Materials: stone/andesite/deepslate mixed for the rocky underside; grass block + dirt transition on top; a few thick hanging roots or vine chains trailing from the underside sells the “torn from the ground” read.
Placement: vary height and distance significantly — stagger both altitude (30–80 blocks) and horizontal distance from camera, not lined up in a row. A small waterfall pouring off an island's edge into open air is a strong, disproportionately effective detail for how simple it is.
9a. Observatory Variant B — The Floating Archway (derived directly from the moodboard art)
A second valid Observatory build, based directly on close re-examination of the original visual reference (both moodboard panels), rather than the text-guide description that produced Sections 1–6 above. This variant reads meaningfully differently from the ground-level pillar-ring plaza: a broken stone ring/archway floating directly among the islands, not connected to a ground valley, with a light beam through its center and a waterfall pouring off it into open air. Both variants are legitimate — build whichever fits the world layout better, or use the archway as a distant landmark visible FROM the ground-level plaza.
The exterior arch structure
Rather than 18 individual freestanding pillars in a closed ring, the art shows a single large, partially-broken stone arch/ring — think a floating Stonehenge fragment on a much larger scale, incomplete rather than a full circle. Suggested approach: build the ring using the same `//cyl` hollow-cylinder technique as Section 6, but at a much larger radius (40–60 blocks) and with 25–35% of the ring deliberately deleted/broken away in 2–3 large gaps, rather than kept fully intact. A vertical light beam through the center (End Rod columns, or beacon beam if a beacon-adjacent mod is available, or simply a tall column of glowstone/sea lantern with glass framing) reads as the structure's active energy signature. Source the waterfall from the structure's base pouring into open air/void — same technique as Section 8's waterfalls, but falling into empty sky rather than a pool, since this variant floats independently of ground terrain.
The interior chamber (18 Energy Sockets)
A separate enclosed space from the exterior arch — the art shows a circular room with a domed or gridded ceiling and 18 individually lit wall-alcoves around the perimeter, each glowing a different color. Suggested build: a circular room 15–20 blocks diameter, stone brick/deepslate brick walls, with 18 recessed alcoves cut into the wall at even 20° spacing (use the same rotate-and-paste technique as Section 6's pillars for perfect spacing). Each alcove holds a single glowing block in its Affinity's color (colored stained glass backed with a light source, or colored concrete + glowstone) — this is the actual buildable expression of the socket-color-lighting mechanic proposed for World Affinity (Supplemental Archive, Provisional Thoughts 4.1) if that mechanic gets locked. Ceiling: a shallow dome using stair/slab blocks, or an exposed “grid” lattice using iron bars/glass panes in a radial pattern, lit from below by the alcoves rather than needing its own light source.
The Resonance Core (centerpiece object)
A distinct interactable centerpiece shown separately in the art — a glowing crystal on a pedestal, positioned at the interior chamber's center or the exterior arch's focal point. Buildable as: a 1–2 block pedestal (chiseled stone brick or polished deepslate base, smooth stone slab top) topped with a large amethyst cluster or a custom glowing block if the eventual Resonance Core/Nexus material (Part 3) gets a real in-world item model. This is the natural in-world anchor point for the actual Observatory game mechanic (Part 1) once implementation begins — the physical thing a player would interact with, distinct from purely decorative geometry.
Status: 🟢 Strong reconstruction — directly derived from the actual visual reference rather than a text description of it. Not yet chosen over the Section 1–6 ground-level variant; both remain valid until a build decision is made.
9b. Observatory Variant C — The Tree-Ring Fusion (Wesley's proposal)
A proposed unification of the World Tree (Section 7) and the floating archway (Section 9a), rather than building them as two separate landmarks in the scene. Confirmed against the actual key-art reference (the poster image, distinct from the two block-concept moodboards): the ground-level Ritual Plaza already sits near the Tree's base in that art, which is exactly the anchor point this fusion needs — the ring doesn't get inserted arbitrarily, it wraps the trunk at a height the Tree's own tapering geometry already supports.
The scale reconciliation
The Tree's trunk (Section 7) tapers from 12–16 blocks diameter at the base to 6–8 blocks by the top of the 60–90 block trunk section. The floating ring (Section 9a) sits at radius 40–60 blocks. Rather than building the ring as a separate structure elsewhere in the sky, wrap it around the trunk at roughly 40–60% of trunk height (~30–50 blocks up), where the trunk has narrowed enough that a 40–60 block ring radius reads as “encircling the tree” rather than “comically oversized ring around a thin pole.” This keeps both structures' established proportions from Sections 6–9a — no rescaling of either one is actually needed, just correct vertical placement of the ring along the trunk's existing taper.
What becomes shared between the two structures
Light source: the ring's central light beam (Section 9a) and the Tree's glowing energy veins (Section 7) become the SAME effect — route the vein channels from the roots up the trunk to the ring's height, where they feed directly into the beam, rather than treating the beam as a separate unrelated light source. One continuous magical throughline instead of two coincidentally-glowing structures near each other.
The interior chamber (18 Energy Sockets, Section 9a) moves INSIDE the trunk itself at the ring's height — hollow out a chamber within the trunk's mass at that point, carve the 18 alcoves into the interior trunk wall rather than a separate stone-brick room. This means the player would climb/access the chamber through the tree's own structure, which is a stronger narrative beat than a detached building floating nearby.
The Resonance Core centerpiece (Section 9a) sits at the exact point where the hollowed trunk chamber meets the ring's center — the vein channels from Section 7 converge there, giving it a genuine reason to be the brightest point in the whole structure rather than an arbitrarily-placed pedestal.
Root buttresses (Section 7) extend down to and connect with the Ritual Plaza's outer ring (Section 6) at ground level — unifying the ground-level plaza-and-pillars version and the floating archway-and-chamber version into ONE structure spanning from ground to mid-canopy, rather than needing to choose between Variant A/B/C for the final build.
Build order implication: construct the Tree's trunk and root buttresses first (Section 7's Phase 1–2 work), THEN build the ring at the correct height as a wrap-around addition once the trunk's taper is already in place — building the ring first and fitting the trunk through it afterward would be far harder to get proportions right. Status: 🔵 Current proposal — a strong, well-scaled synthesis of Variants A and B rather than a competing third option; likely the actual final build direction if the ground-plaza-plus-floating-archway combination appeals visually once test-built.
10. For the Actual Screenshot
Once built: a shader pack, a clear-sky/golden-hour time of day (/time set day then wait for late afternoon, or /time set 4000 for a sunset-adjacent angle), F1 to hide HUD before F2. A well-chosen static angle from slight elevation (standing on a nearby rise, not flat ground) will match a key-art composition better than eye-level.
Status: 🟢 Strong reconstruction — practical, actionable build guidance, not a game-mechanical decision requiring the same lock/candidate vocabulary as design content. Treat as a living reference to build from, refine, and deviate from as needed once actual construction starts.
Zone | Suggested footprint | Notes
World Tree | ~180–220 blocks tall, 40–60 block canopy | The visual anchor — everything else should read as smaller than it
Restoration Observatory | ~45–55 block diameter | Flat or gently raised, near the tree's base
Waterfall/river valley | ~150–250 blocks long, winding | Runs from the tree's base through the whole scene toward the town
Floating islands | 3–5 islands, 15–30 blocks each | Staggered heights 30–80 blocks above the main scene
Town | ~80–100 block cluster | 10–20 small-to-medium buildings along the riverbank
Ruins & bridges | Distributed throughout | 2–4 bridges (15–40 blocks each) plus scattered fragments linking zones
Full scene footprint | ~300–400 blocks edge to edge | Use forced perspective (below) if this is more scale than you want to commit to
Zone | Primary materials | Accent/detail materials
World Tree | Dark oak log (trunk), dark oak leaves (canopy) | Stripped dark oak, spruce log (bark variation), amethyst blocks/clusters (glow veins), moss block, flowering azalea leaves, glow lichen
Restoration Observatory | Stone bricks, mossy stone bricks | Cracked/chiseled stone bricks, smooth stone slabs (dais), 18 colors of concrete/terracotta (one per Affinity, pool rims), vines
Waterfall/river valley | Water source blocks, stone | Mossy cobblestone, prismarine, lily pads, kelp/seagrass, soul campfire (mist effect)
Floating islands | Stone, andesite, deepslate (underside mass) | Grass block, dirt, hanging roots, flowers/small trees, vines
Town | Oak/spruce planks and logs, cobblestone (foundations) | Stone brick (older buildings), lanterns/glowstone behind glass, hay bales (thatch roofing), fences, flower pots
Ruins & bridges | Stone bricks matching the Observatory's crumbling mix | Cobblestone, andesite, oak planks + fences (bridges), moss, vines
